For now, the updated rules are in the character sheet: talents, the Technique builder, weapons and modules are already aligned.

Chapter 1 ·

Welcome to the Grand Line

1.1 · What is Grand Line Chronicles?

Grand Line Chronicles is a tabletop roleplaying game inspired by the world of One Piece, designed to let every player create their own pirate, join a crew and experience an adventure built together with the other players.

Every character begins as more than a simple combination of statistics. They have an Ambition, an origin, a Race, particular abilities, a role within the crew and their own way of facing the world. Some fight on the front line, some helm the ship, some cook, some treat the wounded, some build machinery, and some interpret traces of the past. The crew works because each person brings something the others lack.

One participant takes on the role of Game Master. The GM describes the world, portrays the characters encountered during the voyage, presents dangers, islands, opponents and opportunities, and determines the consequences of players' actions when the rules require a decision.

There is no predetermined path to follow. Players declare what their characters want to do, the Game Master describes what happens and, when the outcome of an action is uncertain, the dice come into play.

The rules of Grand Line Chronicles therefore answer a simple question:

“What happens when we do not already know how things will turn out?”

Fighting an opponent, performing an impossible manoeuvre during a storm, administering desperate treatment, building a weapon, preparing a banquet or trying to cross an unknown island can all become moments in which the character's abilities and the dice roll determine the outcome.

Grand Line Chronicles does not, however, separate the character from crew life. The game also includes what happens between battles: managing the ship, the Galley, the Workshop, the market, resources collected during the voyage and the growth of the different crew members.

Characters do not grow only by becoming stronger in combat. They can become better navigators, cooks capable of supporting the whole crew, Engineers capable of building and maintaining tools, machinery and structures, or fighters capable of developing ever more extraordinary Techniques, Haki and abilities.

At the heart of the game, however, is the crew.

A single character can be exceptional. A complete crew can cross the Grand Line.

1.2 · How to play

A game of Grand Line Chronicles begins with a conversation.

The Game Master describes a situation, players decide how their characters react, and the world responds to their actions. As long as what happens is clear, there is no need to interrupt the story with a die roll.

A pirate opens an unlocked door? They open it.

The cook prepares a simple dinner with ordinary ingredients and all the time they need? There is no need to test their ability.

The navigator consults a familiar route in calm conditions? The voyage can simply continue.

Dice come into play when an action's outcome is uncertain, when there is a real possibility of failure or when the result can have significant consequences.

The basic cycle of play is therefore:

The GM describes the situation → the player declares what they want to do → decide whether a roll is needed → apply the abilities involved → roll the dice → the result changes the situation.

From that point, play continues from the new reality created by the outcome.

If a pirate tries to leap from one ship to another during a storm, the roll does more than determine whether they “succeed” or “fail”. The result can determine how they succeed, which position they reach, which risk they avoid or which problem arises from the attempt.

Likewise, in combat, an attack is not an isolated event. Its result can deal damage, move an enemy, apply a condition, activate a Talent, prepare a subsequent attack or completely change the combatants' positions.

The rules turn these consequences into something clear and shared.

When not to roll

Not every action requires a check.

If there is no real uncertainty, the GM can simply describe the result.

A roll should be used when at least one possible consequence is significant. Failure must be capable of changing something.

This prevents the game from becoming an endless sequence of dice rolls and keeps attention on the moments that truly matter.

Even a highly experienced character can face a situation difficult enough to require a check. Likewise, a simple situation may not require one at all, regardless of the values of their characteristics.

Declaring an action

When a player wants to do something, they should first describe the character's intention, rather than choosing a rule directly.

“I want to run along the parapet and reach the Marine before they close the gate.”

is different from:

“I roll Acrobatics.”

The first declaration tells the table what the character is actually trying to do. Only then do you determine which Attribute, Skill, Technique or other ability best represents that action.

This lets the same system handle very different situations without turning every scene into a list of buttons to press.

The character's abilities

Every pirate has various tools for dealing with what they encounter.

Attributes and Skills represent their general abilities.

Race, Roles and Talents define more specific characteristics.

Techniques represent particular actions developed by the character.

Weapons, Haki, Devil Fruits, equipment and other abilities can further modify what they can do.

Not all of these components contribute to the same roll. The rules in subsequent chapters explain when and how they can be used.

The result must change something

After a roll, the game should not return to exactly where it began.

A success allows the character to obtain what they sought or move closer to their goal.

A failure introduces a consequence, a cost or a new difficulty, or leaves room for the opponent's response.

The same principle holds in combat: each action progressively changes the battle by consuming resources, causing damage, changing positions or creating opportunities.

The die does not narrate the scene in the players' place.

It decides where the story must take a direction.

1.3 · The role of players and the Game Master

In Grand Line Chronicles, everyone helps build the story, but not everyone has the same task.

The players portray the crew members.

The Game Master portrays the rest of the world.

This simple division supports the entire game.

The players

Each player controls their own character.

They decide:

  • what they think;
  • what they say;
  • what they try to do;
  • how they react to events;
  • which abilities to use;
  • which resources to spend;
  • which risks to accept.

The character belongs to the player.

The Game Master can describe what they perceive, what they suffer and the consequences of their actions, but should not decide for the player what they think, feel or choose to do.

A defeat can force a pirate to the ground.

An enemy ability can prevent them from moving.

A situation can make it impossible for them to reach an objective.

But as long as the character retains the ability to choose, that choice remains in the player's hands.

The crew

Grand Line Chronicles is built around different characters sharing the same adventure.

Not everyone needs to be an excellent fighter.

A character can stand out because they know how to navigate, build, heal, cook, interpret ancient evidence or hold the crew together in its darkest moments.

This means the game should not always revolve around the same type of check.

A good adventure alternates situations in which different crew members can make the difference.

During a storm, the Navigator might guide everyone.

After a battle, the Doctor might prevent the consequences from becoming permanent.

A seemingly useless object might become valuable in an Engineer's hands.

An incomprehensible inscription might change the meaning of an entire island thanks to the Archaeologist.

In combat, the same logic still applies: different characters face the battle with different tools.

The crew is not a collection of sheets.

It is a crew.

The Game Master

The Game Master is the voice of the world.

They describe places, people, dangers, creatures and consequences.

They portray:

  • allies;
  • enemies;
  • Marines;
  • rival pirates;
  • island inhabitants;
  • animals and creatures;
  • organisations;
  • everything that does not belong directly to the players.

They are also responsible for presenting situations that require decisions.

They do not determine how the story will end.

They must create something the players can react to.

An island can have an ongoing conflict.

An antagonist can have a plan.

A storm can be approaching.

A rival crew can be seeking the same treasure.

But what happens depends on what the characters do.

The GM as referee

When a situation is clearly covered by the rules, the Game Master applies them.

When something arises that the system does not describe exactly, the GM decides how to represent it using the existing rules.

They can determine:

  • whether a roll is needed;
  • which ability is most appropriate;
  • which difficulty represents the situation;
  • what consequences a failure brings;
  • how to interpret an unusual circumstance.

This authority does not exist to set the Game Master against the players.

It lets the game continue even when someone tries something that no manual could anticipate.

The GM does not play against the crew

The Game Master controls the opponents, but this does not mean their objective is to defeat the players.

An enemy may want to win.

The GM does not.

Their task is to have that enemy act consistently with who they are.

A proud fighter might accept a head-on confrontation.

A sniper might avoid any close confrontation.

An officer might order a retreat if the situation becomes desperate.

A hungry creature might stop fighting as soon as it finds something more interesting to devour.

Opponents have objectives.

The Game Master portrays them.

Information and knowledge

Player and character do not always have the same information.

A player might immediately realise that a particular creature is dangerous.

Their character might never have seen it.

Likewise, the Game Master knows what is behind a door, but the inhabitants of the world do not necessarily know.

Separating what the player knows from what the character knows helps keep the story coherent.

When there is doubt, the GM can ask for an appropriate check to determine how much the character actually knows.

Rules and fiction

The rules represent what happens in the world.

They are not two separate layers.

If a Technique pushes an opponent three metres, that enemy is actually hurled backwards in the story.

If a character runs out of Stamina, that expenditure represents exertion with concrete consequences.

If the ship takes damage, that damage exists in the world and can require repairs.

For this reason, a good description must not contradict the result of the rules.

It must turn that result into something the table can imagine.

A shared responsibility

The Game Master builds the world.

The players give it direction.

The rules determine what happens when neither can simply decide the outcome.

When these three elements work together, the game ceases to be a story told to a group of people.

It becomes a story that nobody at the table could have written alone.

1.4 · The Will of D. System

The mechanical heart of Grand Line Chronicles is the Will of D. System.

Whenever an action's outcome is uncertain, the game translates the character's ability, experience and specialisation into dice.

The fundamental scale is:

d4 → d6 → d8 → d10 → d12 → d20

It does not merely represent higher probabilities. Above all, it represents how competent a character is at something.

A pirate with a low value can still try a difficult action. A character with a high value, however, faces that same situation with better tools and a much more concrete chance of prevailing.

The system therefore does not use large quantities of numerical modifiers that must constantly be added together. The main form of growth directly advances the die associated with an ability.

Grades

Each step on the scale represents a different level of competence.

d4 · Basic
The character has elementary knowledge or an ability that is still underdeveloped.

d6 · Competent
They can use that ability reliably in ordinary situations.

d8 · Expert
They have developed a genuine specialisation.

d10 · Master
The ability has become one of their main strengths.

d12 · Legend
Very few individuals reach this level.

d20 · Mythic
This represents abilities beyond the normal scale, worthy of the Grand Line's most extraordinary characters.

These Grades appear in almost every part of the game.

Attributes, Skills, Techniques, weapons, Haki, Devil Fruits and numerous other systems use the same scale.

The player therefore needs to learn just one mathematical language, which they will encounter everywhere.

Building a roll

A check can use several dice when different abilities contribute to the same action.

For example, a character might tackle a situation using an Attribute together with an appropriate Skill.

In combat, a Technique can become part of the roll.

A weapon can use its own Grade.

Other systems can add dice or modify how they are rolled.

The exact composition depends on the situation and is explained in the relevant chapters.

The principle, however, always remains the same:

each die must represent something the character is actually using.

You do not add a die because “a bonus is needed”.

You add it because a concrete ability contributes to the action.

Additional dice with no specified type

When a rule grants +1 die or several additional dice without specifying their type, use the die that serves as the main reference for the action or effect.

In a normal Check based on Attribute + Skill, the reference die is normally the Attribute unless the ability states otherwise.

If, instead, the action or effect explicitly uses a Skill, Weapon Grade, Technique Die, Damage Die, Fruit Die, Haki Die or another specific die as its reference, the additional die is a new copy of that die.

A generic +1 die does not mean repeating the entire pool and does not automatically add all the dice making up the action.

Additional dice obtained through Prestige are not duplicated by this rule unless explicitly stated.

Die reductions

When a rule states −1 die, a die is not physically removed from the pool. Instead, the reference die is reduced by one Grade along the normal scale:

d20 → d12
d12 → d10
d10 → d8
d8 → d6
d6 → d4

The d4 is the minimum limit and cannot be reduced further by a normal Grade reduction.

If a formula uses several copies of the same reference die, the reduction changes the Grade of that reference. For example, 3 d10 Weapon Dice become 3d8.

The reduction lasts for the period indicated by the effect causing it.

The die represents competence

Imagine two pirates trying to reach the top of a tower.

The first is strong but has little training in acrobatic movement.

The second has exceptional control of their body.

They might attempt the same action, but not necessarily using the same tools.

One might rely on the strength of their arms.

The other might exploit balance and technique.

The system therefore does not seek to impose a single correct solution.

It seeks to represent how the character approaches the problem.

This distinction becomes even more important when Roles, Talents and Techniques come into play.

Two characters with similar values can behave completely differently because they have developed different abilities.

Overcoming a difficulty

When a check is not directly opposed by another character, the Game Master can set a Threshold.

The roll's result is compared with that difficulty.

A simple situation requires a relatively low Threshold.

A risky feat requires a higher Threshold.

Extraordinary actions can require equally extraordinary abilities.

The Threshold does not represent how much the Game Master wants the character to fail.

It represents how difficult the attempted action is.

Opposed checks

When two characters act directly against one another, both can take part in the resolution.

A pirate tries to grab someone who wants to break free.

Two fighters try to act before each other.

A character tries to deceive someone who is trying to understand their intentions.

In these situations, the difficulty is not necessarily fixed.

There is another will on the other side of the roll.

The specific rules establish which dice are used and how the winner of the contest is determined.

Combat uses this same philosophy, but with more precise procedures.

Saves

A Save represents an attempt to resist, avoid or escape an effect. When the ability specifies which Attribute to use, use that Attribute; otherwise the Game Master chooses the most appropriate one.

The Condition establishes the Save's Attribute; the source establishes the Threshold. When the effect comes from a source with a Grade, the source does not roll dice: its Grade determines a fixed Threshold. The target rolls only their complete Attribute, including any Prestige die, and succeeds if the result meets or exceeds the Threshold.

Source GradeThreshold
d43
d64
d85
d106
d127
d2011

Saves and Prestige

A source that has reached Prestige uses its complete Grade to determine the fixed Threshold. Prestige progression and rules are described in the Prestige Manual.

Source GradeThreshold
d20+d4 · Prestige I13
d20+d6 · Prestige II14
d20+d8 · Prestige III15
d20+d10 · Prestige IV16
d20+d12 · Prestige V17
d20+d20 · Saikyō21

Determining the source

SourceGrade used for the Threshold
Effect included in a TechniqueGrade of the Technique containing the effect.
Racial TechniqueCurrent Grade of the Racial Technique.
Talent or Signature that directly applies a ConditionCurrent Grade of the Skill governing the Talent or Signature, unless specifically stated otherwise; at Prestige, use the complete Skill pool.
Fruit effect not included in a TechniqueFruit Die. An effect included in a Fruit Technique instead uses the Technique's Grade.
Direct Haki effectGrade of the Colour producing the effect, when that Colour has a Grade.
Special weapon effectThe weapon's actually usable Grade, unless the effect has its own Threshold.
Musician SongThe instrument's actually usable Grade, unless a specific Prestige evolution applies.
Item, preparation, poison or device with its own ThresholdThreshold written on the sheet: the specific rule takes precedence.
Environmental effect without a GradeThreshold set by the rule or by the Game Master before the roll.

The source Grade cannot be freely chosen from the character's statistics: use the component that mechanically contains or generates the Condition. If a specific rule indicates a different source, that rule takes precedence.

When a Signature or Talent has a specific rule identifying the Technique that generated the effect as its source, that specific rule takes precedence. Breakthrough Strike therefore uses the Role Skill on basic attacks and the Technique Grade when triggered by a Striker Technique.

An effect's own Threshold, an opposed check or a specific procedure takes precedence over the general table. Poisons, substances, physical restraints and environmental conditions retain their sheet's procedure or the one established by the Game Master before the roll.

If an effect grants a Save without specifying its timing, the first Save is made when the Condition is applied. If the Condition persists, the target can repeat it at the end of each of their turns, unless stated otherwise. The Threshold remains the one determined by the original source for as long as that specific effect continues to cause the Condition. New applications follow the normal rules for repeated Conditions and durations (§6.13).

Margin matters

In Grand Line Chronicles, success or failure is not the only thing that matters.

In many situations, how far the result exceeds the required value also matters.

A narrow victory and an overwhelming victory need not produce the same result.

This difference is called the Margin.

The Margin can trigger additional effects, special abilities or better results.

It is particularly important in combat, because some abilities respond directly to the quality of the hit.

Advantage and Disadvantage

Some situations can make an action especially favourable or especially difficult.

The system uses Advantage and Disadvantage to represent these conditions without turning them into a cascade of small modifiers.

Position, Talents, preparation, battlefield conditions and other abilities can grant them.

They do not simply mean that a character “is stronger”.

They mean that, at that precise moment, circumstances are working in their favour or against them.

The detailed rules for rolling and combining them are described in the chapter on the fundamental rules.

Resolving Advantage and Disadvantage

Advantage and Disadvantage apply to the entire dice pool of the roll indicated by the effect.

With Advantage, build the complete pool normally, roll it twice and keep the higher total.

With Disadvantage, build the complete pool normally, roll it twice and keep the lower total.

You therefore do not reroll a single die in the pool: the entire roll is repeated.

Multiple sources of Advantage do not produce further rolls. Likewise, multiple sources of Disadvantage do not stack.

If the same roll has both Advantage and Disadvantage, the two effects cancel each other out and the roll is made normally.

One scale, many systems

The strength of the Will of D. System lies in its continuity.

The same language that describes a Skill also describes a Technique, a weapon, Haki or a Fruit's power.

When a character grows, they do not suddenly enter a second game system.

They continue using the same dice, but combine them in increasingly sophisticated ways.

A new player can therefore begin by learning something very simple:

the larger the die, the higher the level of the ability it represents.

Everything else starts there.

1.5 · Dice and Grades

Grand Line Chronicles uses a single scale to represent how developed an ability is:

d4 → d6 → d8 → d10 → d12 → d20

Each die corresponds to a Grade.

DieGradeMeaning
d4BasicKnowledge or ability that is still elementary
d6CompetentEnough proficiency to use the ability confidently
d8ExpertA clearly established specialisation
d10MasterAn ability refined to an exceptional level
d12LegendA level reached by very few
d20MythicAn ability beyond the normal scale, belonging to the most extraordinary figures

The die and Grade therefore indicate the same position in the progression.

Writing d10 Technique means that the Technique is of Master Grade.

A d12 weapon is a Legend Grade weapon.

A d20 Attribute has reached Mythic Grade.

This language is used throughout the game.

Reading a die

The letter d simply means die.

The number indicates how many sides it has.

d4 therefore means a four-sided die.

d10 is a ten-sided die.

d20 is a twenty-sided die.

When a rule asks you to roll several dice, they are written alongside one another or with the number of copies of the same die that must be rolled.

For example:

d20 + d12

means rolling one twenty-sided die and one twelve-sided die and adding their results.

3d8

means rolling three eight-sided dice.

How these dice are obtained depends on the rule resolving the action.

Grade is not a character level

A character is not simply “d10 level”.

Each of their components can be at a different Grade.

A pirate might have:

  • d12 Strength;
  • d20 Technique;
  • a d10 Skill;
  • a d12 weapon;
  • d8 Armament Haki;
  • a d20 Technique.

This allows strongly specialised characters without forcing every ability to grow at the same time.

A great swordsman can be mediocre at Medicine.

An exceptional Navigator can have modest combat abilities.

A pirate can reach extraordinary levels with their Haki without being equally developed in every other field.

The sheet therefore shows where the character's strength lies, rather than just how powerful they are overall.

Advancing a Grade

Progression always follows the same order:

d4 → d6 → d8 → d10 → d12 → d20

When an ability increases by one Grade, it moves to the next die on the scale.

For example:

d8 → d10

or:

d12 → d20

There are no intermediate Grades between these values.

The move from d12 to d20 is therefore a single advancement on the system's scale, even though the die's numerical size increases much more than the others.

This jump deliberately represents the transition from Legend to Mythic.

The d20 is not simply “a larger die”

The first five Grades describe progressive growth.

The d20, however, represents entry into the system's most extreme tier.

This is where abilities begin appearing for characters who can radically change a battle's outcome, dominate entire situations or use the most advanced forms of their powers.

For this reason, many parts of Grand Line Chronicles reserve specific effects, Talents or abilities for d20 Grade.

Reaching d20 does not, however, mean automatically becoming unbeatable.

A character still depends on:

  • which abilities they have actually developed;
  • which resources they have;
  • which Talents they have chosen;
  • how they use their Techniques;
  • which tools they have available;
  • what their opponent is doing.

Grade measures the power of a component.

It does not determine the scene's outcome on its own.

Grades and requirements

In some parts of the game, Grade does more than determine which die to roll.

It can also represent a requirement.

A weapon can require that the Attribute used has reached a certain Grade.

A Talent can become available only from a certain tier.

An ability can unlock when Haki, a Fruit or another characteristic reaches a precise step.

In these cases, the die also measures the preparation needed to use something correctly.

Individual sections of the manual always indicate when such a requirement exists.

A common scale

Using the same progression in different systems makes a sheet quick to read.

If you see:

d6, you know that the ability is still relatively limited.

If you see:

d12, you know that you are facing something exceptional.

If you see:

d20, that component has entered the Mythic tier.

This applies whether you are discussing an Attribute, a Skill, a Technique, a weapon, Haki or another system that uses Grades.

It is one of the fundamental principles of Grand Line Chronicles:

learn a scale once, then encounter it throughout the game.

1.6 · The Golden Rule

Grand Line Chronicles provides rules for resolving actions, combat, abilities, Techniques, Haki, equipment and most situations that can arise during a session.

But no ruleset can anticipate every idea that will emerge at the table.

Sooner or later, someone will try something that is not written in any table.

When this happens, the Golden Rule comes into play:

if a situation is not clearly covered by the rules, use the simplest solution consistent with what is happening in the scene.

The aim is not to find the perfect rule.

It is to let play continue without breaking the pace.

The situation first, then the rule

When a player describes an action, the table should start from what the character is actually trying to do.

Only then should you look for the most appropriate mechanic.

If a rule already exists and clearly describes that situation, use it.

If several rules seem applicable, choose the one that best represents the action.

If no rule fits, the Game Master can build a resolution using the system's fundamental tools:

  • Attributes;
  • Skills;
  • Thresholds;
  • opposed checks;
  • Advantage and Disadvantage;
  • resources;
  • narrative consequences.

The solution should be simple enough to explain to the table in a few seconds.

Do not add complexity without reason

When a new situation arises, the response should not be to immediately create a new subrule.

If a problem can be resolved with a normal check, that is often the best solution.

If it can be represented with Advantage or Disadvantage, you do not necessarily need to invent a modifier.

If an existing ability already describes something very similar, it can be used as a reference.

The system must support the scene, not slow it down.

Consistency matters

The Golden Rule does not mean identical situations should be resolved completely differently every time.

When the table makes a decision about an unanticipated situation, it is useful to remember it and apply the same logic when that circumstance arises again.

This creates natural continuity.

Players gradually learn how the world works and can make decisions knowing what to expect.

The GM does not change a rule mid-action

When an ability has a clear rule, that rule should be known before the player decides to use it.

Changing how a Technique, Talent or other ability works after the player has already spent a resource makes informed decisions difficult.

If a problem emerges during the session, the GM can make a temporary decision to keep the scene moving.

The issue can then be discussed at the end of the session and, if necessary, clarified for future games.

Abilities do what they say

When a rule describes a precise effect, there is no need to search for hidden meanings.

If a Technique pushes a target 3 metres, it pushes them 3 metres.

If a Talent triggers once per turn, it cannot trigger a second time in the same turn unless another rule explicitly allows it.

If an ability requires a condition, that condition must be met.

This principle lets combinations of abilities work without every interaction needing to be individually rewritten.

Exceptions must be stated.

Specific rules take precedence over general rules

Grand Line Chronicles uses many abilities that modify the normal rules.

When a specific rule contradicts a general rule, the specific rule takes precedence.

For example, if a particular action normally requires a Reaction but a Talent explicitly says it can be performed without spending one, apply the Talent.

If an ability allows you to ignore a defence that would normally apply, that ability is an exception to the general rule.

This hierarchy avoids having to repeat all the rules modified by an ability every time.

When in doubt

When a situation remains ambiguous even after consulting the rules, the GM makes a decision for that scene.

The decision should respect three principles:

Consistency
The solution must make sense in relation to the situation and the existing rules.

Simplicity
It must be quick enough not to stall the session.

Transparency
Players must know how it will be resolved before committing significant resources, whenever possible.

The Golden Rule does not replace the ruleset.

It covers the inevitable gap between what the manual can anticipate and what the players can invent.

Because in Grand Line Chronicles, there is always the possibility that someone will try something nobody thought to write down.

And that is usually where the session becomes interesting.

Chapter 2 ·

Creating your Pirate

Creating a character in Grand Line Chronicles means deciding who they are before establishing how strong they are.

The sheet is built in layers. Each choice adds something different: identity, origin, abilities, role in the crew, fighting style and opportunities for growth.

The first step is the most important.

2.1 · Ambition

Every pirate has a dream.

In Grand Line Chronicles, this dream is called Ambition.

Ambition represents what drives the character to set sail, cross dangerous seas, face superior opponents and continue moving forward when stopping would be easier.

It can be something enormous:

“I will become the world's greatest swordsman.”

or something deeply personal:

“I will find my brother.”

It can concern freedom, knowledge, revenge, power, discovery, protection, glory, their crew or any other goal important enough to give direction to the character's life.

In the character creator, Ambition is simply:

the dream that drives the pirate.

Ambition is not a statistic

Ambition has no die and does not automatically grant numerical bonuses.

It does not increase Attributes, Skills, HP or Stamina simply because it is written on the sheet.

Its value comes from how the character lives it during the adventure.

An ignored Ambition remains merely a sentence.

An Ambition that influences decisions, risks and sacrifices becomes a concrete part of the game.

Acting according to your Ambition

When a character takes an important action guided by their Ambition, the Game Master can decide to reward them if they consider the situation narratively consistent and meaningful.

This reward is not automatic and follows no fixed formula.

Depending on the situation, the GM can grant:

  • a bonus to a check;
  • Advantage;
  • a resource or recovery;
  • an additional reward;
  • a narrative opportunity;
  • a temporary benefit;
  • or any other effect consistent with what is happening.

The aim is not to reward a player every time they mention their Ambition.

The reward should come when the character truly acts in pursuit of it, especially when that choice entails a risk, sacrifice or significant consequence.

A character with the Ambition:

“I will build the fastest ship on the Grand Line.”

might decide to face an extremely dangerous area to recover a unique material needed for their project.

If the GM considers that choice an important moment in their journey, they can decide to grant something in return.

Ambition thus becomes a tool through which narrative and mechanics can meet.

Ambition guides the story

Ambition helps the player understand what truly matters to their character.

It also helps the Game Master create situations with personal significance for the crew.

A place, character or object can take on a completely different value when it comes into contact with someone's dream.

For this reason, Ambition can influence:

events, rewards, encounters, opportunities and narrative development.

It does not, however, oblige the Game Master to build the entire campaign around the characters' dreams.

It is a compass, not a rail.

Writing a good Ambition

A good Ambition should be clear enough for the table to understand what the character wants.

“I want to become strong” is very generic.

“I want to become strong enough to defeat the man who destroyed my village” immediately creates a direction.

“I want to explore the world” works.

“I will draw a complete map of the Grand Line”, however, offers an even more recognisable objective.

An Ambition does not need to be easy to achieve.

It can drive an entire campaign.

Ambition and Background

Background tells where the character comes from.

Ambition tells where they want to go.

The two can be directly connected.

Background: grew up in a village destroyed by a storm.

Ambition:
“I will build a ship that can cross any sea.”

Or they can be completely independent.

Background: child of a merchant family.

Ambition:
“I will discover whether an island in the sky really exists.”

Both possibilities are valid.

Ambition can change

A character can change during the voyage.

They can fulfil their dream, lose it, discover it was not what they truly wanted or find something even more important.

In these cases, Ambition can also evolve.

Achieving it does not necessarily mean abandoning the character.

It can represent the end of one chapter and the beginning of the next.

In Grand Line Chronicles, Ambition does more than explain why a pirate set sail.

It reminds you why they keep sailing.

2.2 · Creation procedure

Creating a pirate in Grand Line Chronicles means building them one element at a time, starting with who they are and progressing to what they can do.

The guided creator follows eight steps:

Concept → Race → Attributes → Role → Skills → Techniques → Fruit → Save

You do not need to know every rule of the game already. Each step defines a precise part of the character and prepares the next.

Step 1 · Concept

The narrative foundations come first.

Here you define:

Name
What the character is called.

Background
Where they come from, what happened to them and which events brought them to the start of the adventure.

Ambition
The dream that drives them to continue the voyage.

Name and Background do not automatically assign mechanical bonuses. Ambition, however, can become relevant during the campaign when the character takes meaningful action to pursue it, as described in the preceding section.

This first step answers the question:

Who is your pirate?

Step 2 · Race

Race establishes some of the character's physical and innate characteristics.

Each Race can determine elements such as:

  • racial Attribute;
  • racial die;
  • starting HP;
  • Hit Die;
  • Movement;
  • racial Technique;
  • special abilities;
  • any limitations.

A Human, a Mink, a Giant and a Fish-Man are not simply four different appearances.

They enter the game with different characteristics and interact with the world differently.

The choice of Race does not, however, determine the character's profession or fighting style.

A Fish-Man can be a Cook.

A Giant can be a Navigator.

A Human can become a Striker.

Race and Role are two separate components.

Step 3 · Attributes

Attributes represent the character's fundamental abilities.

They define how developed they are in different areas and contribute to a great many rolls in the system.

During creation, starting dice are assigned to Attributes according to the creator's rules.

Race can influence one of them through its racial Attribute.

Attributes do not describe what the character has learnt to do.

They describe the foundation on which those abilities are built.

Great Strength does not automatically make someone an Athletics expert.

A high Technique does not automatically grant every ability requiring precision.

For this reason, Attributes and Skills remain separate.

Step 4 · Role

Role represents what the character brings to the crew.

It can indicate their fighting style, their profession aboard ship or both.

Grand Line Chronicles allows characters with two Roles, enabling combinations such as:

  • Fighter + Cook
  • Navigator + Captain
  • Doctor + Fighter

and many others.

Some Roles also have Styles.

Fighter, for example, does not on its own describe how the character approaches a battle. Their Style defines their specialisation.

Likewise, other Roles can have different internal paths.

The Role also assigns a Role Skill, representing the main competence associated with that profession or specialisation.

Race tells what you are.

Role tells your place in the crew.

Step 5 · Skills

Skills represent what the character has learnt to do.

Navigation, Athletics, Medicine, Cooking, Acrobatics, Archaeology and the other Skills distinguish characters who might have similar Attributes but completely different experiences.

The Role already provides its characteristic Skill.

During creation, the character's other competencies are then defined according to the options offered by the creator.

When a check uses a Skill, it is normally combined with the appropriate Attribute dictated by the situation.

Attributes therefore represent basic ability.

Skills represent training.

Step 6 · Techniques

Techniques are the character's characteristic actions.

They do not simply represent a stronger attack.

They can be strikes, defensive manoeuvres, movement, enhancements, area abilities or other actions built through the Technique system.

Every character also has their own racial Technique, linked to the chosen Race.

During creation, the pirate's first personal Techniques are defined.

They can subsequently be developed, improved and used as the foundation for far more advanced combinations, including Special Moves.

Techniques are covered in a dedicated chapter.

Step 7 · Devil Fruit

A Devil Fruit is optional.

Not every character has one, and one is not necessary to reach the highest levels of the game.

If the character has eaten a Fruit, this step defines the information needed for their power.

The Fruit introduces unique possibilities, but also specific weaknesses.

Its presence must also be agreed with the Game Master.

A character without a Fruit simply skips this step.

Haki and Fruit do not represent the same form of progression and are explained separately in their respective chapters.

Step 8 · Save

Once the starting choices are complete, the character is saved and becomes a usable sheet.

From this point, they are no longer simply a character being created.

They enter the Grand Line Chronicles management system, where they can grow during the campaign.

Their sheet will progressively collect:

Attributes, Skills, HP, Stamina, Talents, Techniques, weapons, Haki, any Devil Fruit, equipment, Special Moves and all other abilities obtained during the voyage.

Creation therefore establishes the starting point, rather than the final character.

Starting equipment

Grand Line Chronicles does not use universal starting equipment.

Berry, weapons, equipment, professional tools, ammunition, materials, medicines and other resources available at creation depend on the campaign.

The Game Master establishes the starting equipment based on the adventure's starting point, the crew's resources and the campaign's tone.

What is calculated automatically

Not every value must be chosen manually.

Some statistics derive directly from earlier decisions.

Race, for example, helps define starting HP, Hit Die and Movement.

Every pirate also begins with 6 Stamina.

Other values are determined by the rules associated with Attributes, Race, Roles and owned abilities.

The creator gathers this information in the sheet, allowing the player to focus on the decisions that truly define the character.

2.3 · Attributes

Attributes represent the character's fundamental abilities.

There are four in Grand Line Chronicles:

AttributeRepresentsUsage examples
StrengthPhysical power, muscles, enduranceHeavy blows, lifting, pushing, taking hits
TechniquePrecision, speed, controlTechnical strikes, precise movements, refined use of abilities
SpiritWillpower, presence, inner strengthHaki, resisting fear, asserting yourself over others
CunningIngenuity, planning, adaptabilityNavigating, devising solutions, deceiving, planning

Attributes do not represent professions or specific training.

That is why Skills exist.

A character can have great Cunning without being an expert Navigator, just as they can have extraordinary Strength without great Athletics.

When an action requires both components, Attribute and Skill can work together.

Strength

Strength represents the character's physical power.

It comes into play when what matters is how much they can push, lift, hit or endure through their body.

It can be used for:

  • attacks based on power;
  • physical grappling;
  • lifting or dragging weights;
  • smashing through obstacles;
  • resisting through their toughness;
  • using weapons or abilities that require great strength.

A character with high Strength is not necessarily slow or lacking technique.

It simply means that, when they choose to rely on physical power, they have a better die.

Technique

Technique represents precision, speed and control.

It is used when the outcome depends more on executing a movement correctly than on the force behind it.

It can represent:

  • precise attacks;
  • combat based on speed and control;
  • refined weapon use;
  • complex movements;
  • abilities requiring coordination;
  • Techniques built around precision.

Strength and Technique can therefore achieve the same goal through different approaches.

A fighter can bring down an opponent by overwhelming them with power or striking the exact spot with extreme precision.

Spirit

Spirit represents willpower, presence and inner strength.

It is the ability to remain steadfast when the pressure is not merely physical.

It can contribute to situations involving:

  • Haki;
  • resisting fear;
  • intimidation or a commanding presence;
  • willpower;
  • abilities that depend directly on the character's determination.

Spirit does not measure how good, brave or morally strong a character is.

It measures how well they can impose and maintain their will.

Cunning

Cunning represents ingenuity, the ability to observe a situation and find a solution.

It can be used when the character needs to:

  • plan;
  • interpret information;
  • find their bearings;
  • devise a strategy;
  • deceive;
  • exploit the environment;
  • solve a problem through reasoning.

A character with high Cunning does not automatically know every discipline.

Their specific knowledge still depends on the appropriate Skills.

Cunning represents the ability to make good use of what they know.

Assigning Attributes at creation

Race determines the first important value.

Each Race assigns its Racial Die to a specific Attribute, or allows a choice among those permitted by the Race.

Once the Racial Die has been assigned, three Attributes remain.

During creation:

one of these is raised to d6;

the other two start at:

d4.

The basic distribution is therefore:

Racial Die + d6 + d4 + d4

For most Races the Racial Die is d8, while some can use different values.

The precise value always depends on the chosen Race.

Example

A character chooses a Race that grants:

d8 Strength

Technique, Spirit and Cunning remain.

The player chooses:

d6 Technique

The starting sheet is therefore:

AttributeDie
Strengthd8
Techniqued6
Spiritd4
Cunningd4

Another character of the same Race could instead choose d6 Spirit and start with a completely different distribution.

Race establishes part of the structure.

The player decides how to build the rest.

Racial Attribute

The Racial Attribute is the Attribute in which the character's nature grants the most significant starting advantage.

It is not necessarily the Attribute they must use throughout the campaign.

A character can freely develop another Attribute over time until it exceeds that one.

The Racial Attribute also determines some interactions with racial characteristics and other derived statistics, which are explained in the appropriate chapters.

Attributes can grow

The values assigned during creation are only the starting point.

During the adventure, Attributes can advance along the normal Grade scale:

d4 → d6 → d8 → d10 → d12 → d20

A character can therefore begin with d4 Technique and, over the course of their story, turn it into one of their main characteristics.

Growth is not automatic.

It requires the resources specified by the advancement system.

The Attribute does not determine the roll on its own

Having a high Attribute does not mean automatically adding it to any vaguely related action.

The die must be consistent with what the character is actually doing.

If a pirate tries to persuade someone with elaborate reasoning, the roll might use different tools from someone seeking the same result by imposing their presence.

The situation determines which Attribute best represents the action.

When a rule explicitly indicates an Attribute, however, use that one.

This is particularly important for Techniques, weapons and special abilities, which can have a precise reference Attribute.

Attributes and character identity

The Attribute distribution already begins to show what kind of pirate you are creating.

High Strength suggests someone who faces obstacles head-on.

High Technique favours precision and control.

High Spirit builds a presence that is difficult to break.

High Cunning makes the character especially suited to reading and manipulating situations.

They are not, however, cages.

Two characters with the same Attributes can become completely different once you add Race, Roles, Skills, Talents and Techniques.

That is where true specialisation begins.

2.4 · Skills

Skills represent what the character has learnt to do.

Attributes describe their fundamental abilities.

Skills instead describe experience, training and specific competencies.

When a check requires a Skill, the roll normally uses:

Attribute + Skill

against a Threshold or another roll, depending on the situation.

The Attribute describes how the character approaches the problem.

The Skill describes what they know how to do.

Available Skills

Grand Line Chronicles uses these Skills:

SkillRepresents
CraftingObjects and tools
MedicineWounds, poisons
NavigationRoutes, voyages
CookingMeals, recovery
StealthMoving unseen, lockpicking
AthleticsRunning, physical exertion, grappling
ArchaeologyHistory, artefacts, Poneglyph
AcrobaticsBalance, agility, evasion
ArtMusic, performances, forgeries
ObservationDangers, details, clues
MechanicsMachinery, devices, cannons
CarpentryShip construction and maintenance
ExplorationMoving and finding your bearings on islands
CommunicationPersuading, negotiating, commanding
NaturePlants, climate, the natural environment
SurvivalFood, shelter, enduring hostile environments

A Skill does not necessarily define a single action.

Observation, for example, can identify an ambush, pick out a detail in a scene or notice something others have overlooked.

Mechanics can concern an engine, a cannon or a complex device.

Context determines which Skill is appropriate.

The Role Skill

Every Role has a Role Skill.

During creation, this starts directly at:

d8

and represents the competence in which the character begins as a true specialist.

For example, a Cook uses Cooking, a Navigator Navigation, and a Doctor Medicine.

Some Styles instead allow their Role Skill to be chosen from several options.

The Role Skill is more than a Skill higher than the others.

Its growth is also linked to the development of the Role and its Talents.

It therefore represents one of the main axes of character progression.

Two Roles, only one starting Role Skill

A pirate can have two Roles, but at creation receives only one Role d8.

The player decides which of the Skills available through their Roles represents their main starting specialisation.

The second Role still remains a full part of the character, allowing access to its abilities and growth path, but does not automatically grant a second free d8.

A Fighter + Cook, for example, must decide where their most developed starting competence lies.

They could start with their Fighter Style's Skill at d8 and develop Cooking during the campaign.

Or do the reverse.

Assigning Skills at creation

Besides the Role Skill at d8, the character chooses:

2 Skills at d6

e:

2 Skills at d4

The starting competence structure is therefore:

QuantityGrade
1d8 · Role Skill
2d6
2d4

The other Skills do not receive a die during creation.

This does not mean the character is unable to perform any action related to those competencies.

It means that they do not have a developed Skill to add to the roll.

When a situation requires specific competencies, the GM establishes whether the action can be attempted without that Skill and under what conditions.

Attribute and Skill are not permanently linked

A Skill does not necessarily belong to only one Attribute.

The combination depends on what the character is actually doing.

A pirate could use:

Cunning + Communication

to build a reasoned negotiation.

In a different situation, they could use:

Spirit + Communication

to assert themselves through presence and determination.

Likewise, Athletics could be associated with Strength in a physical confrontation, while another situation might require a different combination.

When a rule expressly states which Attribute to use, that instruction takes precedence.

Creation example

A character chooses Navigator as their main Role.

Their Role Skill is therefore:

d8 Navigation

They then decide to be particularly attentive and accustomed to life in the wild:

d6 Observation
d6 Survival

Finally, they choose two competencies that are still basic:

d4 Communication
d4 Nature

The character therefore starts as an expert Navigator, with good survival and perception abilities, but still has plenty of room to grow in other fields.

Skill growth

Skills can advance along the same scale used by the rest of the system:

d4 → d6 → d8 → d10 → d12 → d20

Over time, a secondary competence can therefore become one of the character's main characteristics.

Growth of the Role Skill is also particularly important, because it allows access to ever higher tiers of the Role's Talent tree.

The character therefore does not grow merely by increasing their numbers.

By growing in Skills, they become able to do new things.

Synergies and Unique Traits

Developing a Skill can create a Synergy with an Attribute. A Synergy is a specific Attribute + Skill pair which, on reaching the required Grades, makes Unique Traits available. A Skill does not have to be a Role Skill to contribute to a Synergy. The full rules, requirements and Unique Trait Catalogue appear in §16.9.

2.5 · Hit Points and Hit Die

Hit Points, abbreviated HP, represent how much a character can endure before being taken out of combat.

Wounds, blows, falls, explosions and other effects can reduce the character's HP.

Starting HP is not the same for everyone.

Race establishes:

  • starting HP;
  • the racial Hit Die.

A Tontatta, a Human and a Giant therefore do not start with the same physical resilience, nor develop their HP at the same rate during the campaign.

Current HP and Maximum HP

The sheet distinguishes between:

Maximum HPThe total Hit Points the character can normally have.
Current HPHow many Hit Points they have at that moment.

When a character takes damage, their Current HP is reduced.

Maximum HP remains unchanged unless a rule modifies it.

For example, a character with:

18 / 30 HP

has 30 Maximum HP, but at that moment only 18 Current HP remain.

Healing and other HP recovery effects cannot normally raise Current HP above Maximum HP unless an ability explicitly states otherwise.

Rules for healing, recovery, critical conditions and reaching 0 HP are covered in the dedicated chapters.

The Hit Die

Every Race has its own Hit Die.

The Hit Die represents the Race's natural resilience and determines how much the character's Maximum HP increases when they receive an Upgrade.

The Hit Dice used by Races are:

d6, d8 e d10

The Hit Die is not rolled during character growth.

Instead, each Upgrade uses a fixed value equal to half the Hit Die's maximum value.

Hit DieHP gained per Upgrade
d6+3 HP
d8+4 HP
d10+5 HP

There is therefore no randomness in Hit Point growth.

Two characters of the same Race who have received the same number of Upgrades gain the same amount of HP through their Hit Die, unless Talents, abilities or other rules directly modify their Maximum HP.

HP growth

With each Upgrade, the character automatically increases their Maximum HP by half the maximum value of their racial Hit Die.

The standard progression is therefore:

d6 → +3 HP per Upgrade
d8 → +4 HP per Upgrade
d10 → +5 HP per Upgrade

For example, a character has:

Maximum HP: 14; Hit Die: d6

At the first Upgrade, they gain:

14 + 3 = 17 HP

At the second:

17 + 3 = 20 HP

At the third:

20 + 3 = 23 HP

Growth is therefore constant and predictable.

Example with a d8 Hit Die

A character starts with:

16 HP; d8 Hit Die

Each Upgrade adds:

+4 HP

Their progression is therefore:

UpgradeMaximum HP
Creation16
1st Upgrade20
2nd Upgrade24
3rd Upgrade28
4th Upgrade32
5th Upgrade36

Example with a Giant

A Giant has:

22 starting HP; d10 Hit Die

With each Upgrade, they gain:

+5 Maximum HP

Their progression is therefore:

22 → 27 → 32 → 37 → 42 → 47...

The racial resilience advantage therefore does not concern only starting HP.

A naturally tougher Race continues to gain more HP during growth, but the difference remains proportionate to the overall progression rate.

The Hit Die is racial

The Hit Die depends on Race, not Role.

Becoming a Fighter, Doctor, Cook or Navigator, or developing a Second Role, does not automatically change your Hit Die.

A Human Fighter and a Human Archaeologist both have:

d6 Hit Die

and therefore normally gain:

+3 HP per Upgrade

What differs between the two characters is how they develop and use their abilities, rather than the natural growth of their Hit Points.

Talents, powers, equipment or other rules can modify Maximum HP, recovery or resilience when they explicitly state this.

Why the Hit Die is not rolled

HP growth should represent the character's development, rather than depend on luck.

If the Hit Die were rolled at each Upgrade, two characters of the same Race and with the same number of advancements could reach the same point in the campaign with very different amounts of HP solely because of their dice results.

Using fixed growth instead:

d6 = +3 HP; d8 = +4 HP; d10 = +5 HP

the Race has a precise, predictable influence on the character's resilience.

The Hit Die thus continues to distinguish more fragile Races from naturally tougher ones without making Hit Points grow faster than other elements of progression.

2.6 · Stamina

Stamina, abbreviated ST, represents the reserve of energy a character can spend to sustain their most demanding abilities.

Enhanced Techniques, Talents, Haki, Special Moves and other effects can require Stamina expenditure.

Every pirate begins their adventure with:

6 maximum ST

The sheet always distinguishes between:

Maximum Stamina
The total amount of ST the character can have.

Current Stamina
The amount of ST still available at that moment.

A character with:

7 / 16 ST

has 16 maximum Stamina, but 7 remain to spend.

Spending Stamina

Every ability using Stamina explicitly states its cost.

If a Technique requires:

5 ST

the character must have at least 5 ST available to use it and subtract them from their reserve.

When several abilities are combined and their rules specify separate costs, those costs are added together.

For example:

Technique: 5 ST
Talent: 2 ST

Total cost:

7 ST

The character cannot spend Stamina they do not have.

Immediate costs and upkeep

Stamina is not always consumed at the same moment.

Some abilities require an immediate cost.

Spend 3 ST to use this ability.

Others can require an ongoing cost:

1 ST per turn to maintain the effect.

In these cases, activation and upkeep must be considered separately.

If an ability remains active for several turns, the character must continue to have the Stamina needed to sustain it.

The individual ability's description always specifies when and how much to pay.

Stamina growth

Stamina increases with Upgrades, but its growth progressively slows.

A young pirate quickly improves their ability to sustain exertion.

Over time, however, increasing that reserve further becomes more difficult.

The standard progression is:

Upgrade tierStamina increase
1-10+1 ST each Upgrade
11-20+1 ST every 2 Upgrades
21-35+1 ST every 3 Upgrades
36++1 ST every 5 Upgrades

Starting from the initial 6 ST, growth produces values such as:

Upgrade reachedMaximum ST
06
17
511
1016
1217
2021
2322
3526
4027
5029

Stamina therefore keeps growing even in very long campaigns, without increasing indefinitely at the same rate.

Exactly when it increases

To make progression easy to follow, increases occur at the following Upgrades:

1-10: at every Upgrade.

11-20: at Upgrades 12, 14, 16, 18 and 20.

21-35: at Upgrades 23, 26, 29, 32 and 35.

From the 36th onwards: at Upgrades 40, 45, 50, 55, 60...

You therefore only need to check the number of Upgrades reached to know whether maximum Stamina increases.

⚓ Note for the Game Master

This progression represents the standard reference for Grand Line Chronicles, rather than a mandatory structure for every campaign.

The Game Master can modify it according to their adventure's length and pace.

A short campaign can use faster Stamina growth. A particularly long campaign can slow it further.

What matters is maintaining a sensible relationship between available Stamina and ability costs, so that spending ST remains a meaningful choice.

Why growth slows

A character's power does not increase only through Stamina.

During the campaign, these also grow:

Attributes, Skills, Techniques, Talents, Haki, Devil Fruits, equipment and other abilities.

Linear Stamina growth would therefore allow more advanced characters not only to have far more powerful abilities, but also to use them constantly.

With slower progression, however, the character becomes more enduring without turning Stamina into a practically inexhaustible resource.

A Technique costing 7 or 8 ST remains significant even for a veteran.

Stamina and power

Stamina does not directly determine an ability's power.

A d20 Technique does not become stronger because the character has 25 ST rather than 15.

A larger reserve, however, allows you to:

use more costly abilities, sustain effects longer and combine more resources before running out.

The difference is fundamental.

Die growth increases power.

Stamina growth increases endurance under exertion.

Techniques and Stamina

A Technique does not necessarily consume Stamina.

The effects added to the Technique determine its cost where applicable.

This allows a character to have both simple, inexpensive abilities and far more demanding moves.

During a battle, they can therefore choose whether to conserve their energy or invest a significant part of their reserve in a decisive attack.

A 2 ST Technique and an 8 ST Technique should not carry the same weight in the player's decision.

Stamina exists precisely to create that difference.

Talents and Stamina

Some Talents require ST expenditure.

When a Talent has a cost, it must be paid as indicated in its description.

Talents with 0 ST, however, do not consume Stamina and can function as passive or conditional abilities according to their own rules.

This becomes particularly important when composing Special Moves, where several elements can contribute to the same use.

Haki and Stamina

Haki uses its own resource called PIP, but some of its abilities can also require Stamina.

The two resources remain separate.

An ability can therefore require, for example:

1 PIP to activate

e:

1 ST per turn to be maintained.

Spending PIP does not automatically mean spending Stamina, and vice versa.

Its full operation is described in the Haki chapter.

When Stamina reaches 0

A character at:

0 ST

is not automatically defeated.

They can still move, act and use every ability that does not require Stamina expenditure.

They cannot, however, use an ability without enough ST to pay its cost.

An ability requiring 3 ST cannot be used with only 2 ST available.

Some effects can also directly reduce Stamina to 0 or impose specific consequences when this happens. In that case, the ability's description takes precedence.

Recovering Stamina

Stamina can be recovered through rest, abilities, meals, Talents, Songs and other effects provided by the game.

When ST is recovered:

Current Stamina cannot normally exceed Maximum Stamina.

If an effect allows this limit to be exceeded, it must explicitly state so.

The different forms of recovery are explained in their respective chapters.

2.6.1 · Rest and recovery

There are two main forms of rest.

Short Rest

A Short Rest can be taken only when the characters are outside immediate danger and can actually interrupt their activities.

During a Short Rest, a character recovers:

  • HP equal to half their Maximum HP;
  • ST equal to half their Maximum Stamina.

Halves are always rounded up.

Recovery cannot raise HP or ST above their respective maximum values.

A Short Rest also recovers resources expressly stated to be restored by it, such as Haki PIP.

Long Rest

A Long Rest requires a genuine period of sleep and is taken outside active scenes.

At the end of a Long Rest, the character fully recovers their HP and ST, as well as other resources that expressly indicate a Long Rest as their recovery condition.

Resting means resting

A character cannot use a Short or Long Rest as working time.

Crafting, repairs, construction, demanding training and other activities requiring continuous action are not performed simultaneously with a Rest.

Abilities that expressly permit particular activities during a Rest constitute an exception.

2.7 · Movement

Movement indicates how many metres a character can travel during their turn.

The value depends mainly on Race and represents elements such as build, stride length, natural agility and particular physical abilities.

The sheet always expresses Movement in metres.

Racial base Movement

RaceMovementSpecial Movement
Human10 m
Fish-Man / Merman10 m20 m in water
Giant14 m
Mink14 m16 m in Sulong
Lunarian10 m
Cyborg10 m
Tontatta8 m

These values represent the Race's natural mobility.

A Giant covers more ground thanks to their stride.

A Tontatta, while potentially extremely agile, has a much smaller body and therefore travels a shorter distance with their normal Movement.

Movement during the turn

During their turn, a character can travel a total distance equal to their Movement value.

A character with:

Movement 10 m

therefore allows movement of up to 10 metres.

They do not have to use it all.

They can move only a few metres, remain still or use their Movement according to the position they want to reach.

When combat permits, Movement can be divided around the character's other actions.

For example, a pirate with Movement 10 m could:

move 4 m → act → move the remaining 6 m.

The total distance used remains:

10 m

There is no universal Run

In Grand Line Chronicles, there is no generic Run action that automatically doubles Movement.

The value on the sheet represents how much ground the character can normally cover during their turn.

To exceed it, they must have something that permits it.

This can be:

Techniques, Talents, racial abilities, transformations, Devil Fruits, Modules, Haki or other specific effects.

This makes mobility a real feature of the build.

A character specialising in movement can reach distances that a normal character cannot cover merely by sacrificing their Action.

Movement and Displacement

It is important to distinguish between Movement and Displacement.

Movement

It is the base value on the sheet.

It indicates how many metres the character can normally travel in their turn.

Displacement

It is movement granted by a specific ability.

A Technique might say:

Dash: move 5 m.

Those 5 metres come from the Technique and follow its rules.

They do not automatically increase the Movement value written on the sheet.

This allows Techniques and Talents to create genuinely more mobile characters without constantly modifying the base statistic.

Example

A Human has:

Movement 10 m

During their turn, they use all 10 metres.

They then activate an ability granting:

Displacement of 5 m

They can therefore travel a total of:

15 m

The first 10 come from their Movement.

The other 5 come from the ability.

Their base Movement still remains:

10 m

Special racial movements

Some Races modify their Movement under particular conditions.

Fish-Man / Merman

On land, they use:

10 m

In water, they use:

20 m

The greater aquatic speed is not additional Displacement.

It replaces the normal Movement value while the character is in the appropriate environment.

Mink

The Mink normally has:

14 m

During Sulong:

16 m

Here too, the special value temporarily replaces the normal one.

Modifying Movement

Some abilities can directly modify the Movement value.

A rule can, for example, say:

Movement +3 m

or:

double Movement

or:

Movement 20 m for this scene

When this happens, the character's base value is temporarily modified as indicated.

This differs from an ability that simply grants additional Displacement.

The distinction is important.

If an ability says:

Movement ×2

it affects the Movement value.

If it says:

move 5 m

it instead grants only that movement.

Outside combat

Movement in metres is particularly useful when precise position matters.

Outside combat, you do not need to measure every second of running.

If a character tries to:

reach a ship setting sail, pursue someone through a city, escape a collapsing building or quickly cross dangerous terrain,

the Game Master can handle the situation narratively or require an appropriate check.

In these cases, abilities such as these can become important:

Athletics, Acrobatics, racial characteristics, Talents or movement Techniques.

The Movement value remains a useful reference, but does not turn every pursuit into a series of turns counted metre by metre.

Difficult terrain and particular movements

Not all movement takes place on clear ground.

Climbing, swimming, moving through rubble, crossing unstable surfaces or jumping between two buildings can require specific rules or checks.

In these cases, the Game Master determines whether normal Movement is sufficient or a check is needed.

The character's abilities can modify these conditions.

A Fish-Man, for example, approaches water completely differently from most other Races.

A Talent might allow a particular type of terrain to be ignored.

A Technique might allow an obstacle to be bypassed entirely through Displacement.

The detailed rules are explored further in the combat chapter.

Movement and growth

Base Movement does not increase automatically with Upgrades.

A character does not become progressively faster simply because they have received more advancements.

Growth in mobility instead comes through what the character develops:

Talents, Techniques, transformations, Fruits, Modules and other abilities.

This keeps the natural difference between Races significant even in advanced campaigns.

2.8 · Passive Defence and Active Defence

Defence represents a character's ability to avoid, block or resist attacks.

Grand Line Chronicles has two main forms of defence:

Passive Defence, always present and with no cost;

Active Defence, a voluntary choice that uses the character's Reaction.

The two defences represent different approaches.

Passive Defence is reliable and automatic.

Active Defence lets you try to neutralise an attack directly, but involves risk.

Passive Defence

Passive Defence is a fixed Threshold.

It requires no rolls, Actions, Reactions or Stamina.

The formula is:

Passive Defence = sum of the maximum values of the Racial Attribute's Grade + 1

Passive Defence and Prestige

When the Racial Attribute enters Prestige, Passive Defence continues to depend on its complete Grade. To determine it, add the maximum values of all dice making up the Racial Attribute's Grade, then add +1. No roll is made.

The complete Passive Defence table, including Prestige Grades, appears in §6.8.

Example: a character with a d20+d8 Racial Attribute has PD 29. With Monstrous Body, PD becomes 31; with Living Fortress, it becomes 33. Living Fortress replaces Monstrous Body's +2 bonus with +4 total: the two values are not added together.

Base Passive Defence remains determined by the Racial Attribute. Any explicit PD modifiers granted by Talents, Haki, Techniques or Unique Traits apply to the final value. Monstrous Body grants +2 PD; Living Fortress raises this bonus to +4, replacing the +2 without stacking with it. Modifiers from different sources combine according to their respective rules; Evolutions replace the previous benefit (§16.9).

For example:

d8 Racial Attribute → Passive Defence 9
d12 Racial Attribute → Passive Defence 13

The Racial Attribute

Passive Defence always uses the Attribute enhanced by Race.

RaceRacial Attribute
Humanchosen by the player
Fish-Man / MermanStrength
GiantStrength
MinkTechnique
LunarianSpirit
CyborgStrength
TontattaStrength or Technique

If the Racial Attribute grows during the campaign, Passive Defence automatically increases too, even beyond d20.

For example:

d8 → Defence 9
d10 → Defence 11
d12 → Defence 13
d20 → Defence 21

Passive Defence does not automatically increase with the number of Upgrades.

Attack against Passive Defence

When a character does not use Active Defence, the attack is compared with their Passive Defence.

To hit:

Attack > Passive Defence

If the result equals Defence:

Defence holds

If it is lower:

the attack misses

Against a target with:

Passive Defence 9

we would therefore have:

AttackResult
8misses
9Defence holds
10hits, margin +1
12hits, margin +3
13hit with margin +4

Attack margin becomes important for various combat rules, including Style Signatures.

Active Defence

Every combatant can attempt Active Defence when targeted by an attack.

Active Defence uses:

1 Reaction

The player must declare it when selected as the target, before the attacker's attack roll.

Rather than relying on their Passive Defence, the character directly attempts to meet or exceed the opponent's roll.

Active Defence is therefore a gamble.

If it succeeds, the attack is completely neutralised.

If it fails, the blow lands.

What to roll

The roll depends on how the character tries to defend themselves.

The two basic forms are:

Active DefenceRoll
DodgeTechnique + Acrobatics
BlockStrength + Athletics

Attack Technique as Active Defence

Besides Dodge and Block, you can use a compatible attack Technique as Active Defence. Declare this choice when selected as a target, before the attacker's attack roll. Spend the Reaction and immediately pay all the Technique's normal ST costs; failure does not refund Stamina. Make the normal Technique roll: Attribute + Technique Die, unless specific rules apply. If you meet or exceed the attacker's roll, you neutralise the attack; ties favour the defender. A Technique used to defend does not normally deal damage or automatically apply its offensive effects. With the Counterblow Talent, only if the defence exceeds the opponent's roll do you also deal the Technique's normal Damage Die. A tie defends but deals no damage.

The player describes their approach before the roll.

Dodge

A Dodge represents physically avoiding the attack's path through speed, coordination and body control.

Roll:

Technique + Acrobatics

It can represent actions such as moving at the last instant, ducking under a blow, twisting out of its path or using agility and timing to avoid being reached.

Block

A Block represents facing the attack directly through strength and physical solidity.

Roll:

Strength + Athletics

It can mean stopping a blow, countering a push, using your body to absorb the impact or opposing force with force.

Exactly how this happens remains narrative, provided it is consistent with the situation.

If you do not have the Skill

Active Defence is available to every combatant.

You therefore do not need Acrobatics or Athletics to attempt it.

If the character lacks the appropriate Skill, they use only the Attribute.

For example, a character without Acrobatics can still attempt a Dodge by rolling:

Technique

A character without Athletics can attempt a Block by rolling:

Strength

Naturally, having and developing the Skill makes Active Defence much more effective.

Declaring Active Defence

When a character is selected as an attack's target, they must decide whether to rely on Passive Defence or use Active Defence before the attacker makes their attack roll.

The defender therefore does not know the attack's result when making this decision.

If they choose Active Defence, they use their Reaction and the defensive roll's result replaces Passive Defence for that attack.

Once the choice has been made, they cannot return to Passive Defence after seeing the results.

Resolving Active Defence

Active Defence ≥ Attack → attack defended
Attack > Active Defence → successful attack

Ties always favour the defender.

If Active Defence fails, Passive Defence is not used as a second protection.

Example: Dodge

An enemy declares an attack. Before the roll, the target chooses to dodge.

The target has:

d8 Technique
d6 Acrobatics

They spend their Reaction. Attack and defence are then rolled.

The attack scores:

14

The target rolls:

d8 + d6

If they score:

14 or more → dodges the attack

If they score:

13 or less → is hit

Example: Block

A character has:

d10 Strength
d8 Athletics

An enemy declares an attack, and the character chooses to block before the roll.

The attack totals:

16

The character rolls:

d10 + d8

With:

16 or more → successful block

With:

15 or less → failed block

Passive Defence or Active Defence

When a character attempts Active Defence, they choose to take a risk.

Passive Defence is not added to the roll.

Nor does it function as a safety net after failure.

If the character chooses to use their Reaction to defend actively:

the Active Defence result replaces Passive Defence for that attack.

If the roll fails, the attack hits.

This prevents Active Defence from becoming merely a free second chance.

Example

A character has Passive Defence 11. When the enemy declares the attack, before the attack roll, they choose to attempt Active Defence and spend their Reaction.

The attack scores 12; Active Defence scores 9. The character is hit and cannot return to Passive Defence 11 after seeing the results.

Active Defence does not deal damage

Normal Active Defence serves only to:

neutralise the attack

A successful Dodge does not automatically hit the enemy.

A successful Block does not automatically deal damage.

Even if the Defence result greatly exceeds the opponent's, basic Active Defence does not produce a free attack.

Talents and Counterattacks

Some Talents and abilities allow further development of Active Defence.

For example, they can turn a successful defence into:

a Counterattack, a control effect, Displacement or another particular consequence.

In these cases, the Talent grants the additional effect.

It is not a property of basic Active Defence.

This allows true defensive specialisations.

One character can simply be good at avoiding blows.

Another can build their style around the idea of:

waiting for the attack, neutralising it and immediately punishing the opening.

Parries and specific abilities

Techniques, Talents, weapons, Haki or other abilities can provide different methods of Active Defence.

An ability might, for example, allow a Parry using a different formula.

When a specific ability indicates which roll to make, that formula replaces the normal one:

Technique + Acrobatics

or:

Strength + Athletics

The specific rule always takes precedence over the general rule.

The Reaction is a resource

Active Defence consumes the character's Reaction.

Once used, the character cannot spend that same Reaction again until it is recovered according to the normal combat rules.

This means a character must choose when taking a risk is truly worthwhile.

They can use their Reaction to stop the first incoming attack.

Or save it, fearing something more dangerous shortly afterwards.

Likewise, if they have already used their Reaction for another ability, they cannot then use normal Active Defence.

Defence therefore becomes a tactical decision, rather than an automatic roll against every attack.

Defence modifiers

Some abilities can temporarily modify Passive Defence:

Passive Defence +2

or:

Passive Defence −2

Others can directly modify Active Defence:

+1 die to Dodge

or:

Advantage on the next Block

The two systems remain distinct.
