Multiple abilities granting the same machine a second activation do not stack.

Unless a rule explicitly states otherwise, a machine cannot gain more than two activations in the same round through these effects.

In naval battle, a system's second activation does not automatically equal a second complete ship manoeuvre.

Signature · Running Like a Dream

Free · Always active

When you achieve a margin of at least +4 on a Mechanics Check made to operate a machine, that machine can activate a second time in the same round.

The second activation follows all the device's normal rules and pays any ammunition, energy or other resources again.

The Signature only applies to the machine that produced the Check.

If the same machine already has a second activation thanks to Fine Tuning · Mastery, Everything at Full Capacity or a similar ability, it gains no further activations.

d8 Talents · Deckhand

  • Rapid Response
  • Action

You can quickly restore a Broken device, cannon or machine to working order.

You must be able to physically reach the machine and access its controls or the affected components.

Suitable tools and, where the damage requires them, ordinary spare parts are necessary.

Make a Cunning + Mechanics Check against the device's repair Threshold.

If the machine has no specific Threshold, use Threshold 8.

On a success, the Fault is removed and the device becomes operational again.

Rapid Response:

  • Does not reconstruct a Destroyed device.
  • Does not automatically recover SP.
  • Does not restore HP to a Cyborg.
  • Does not automatically repair a Damaged body Module.

Cyborgs and body Modules follow the specific procedures in the Cyborg Manual and the abilities that concern them.

  • Dismantler
  • Passive

When salvaging mechanical components from machines, wrecks or Cyborgs, add your Mechanics Die to the roll.

  • Fine Tuning · Basic
  • Passive

When you personally operate a cannon or a machine, you improve its efficiency.

If the result produced by the device is expressed in dice, add +1 die of the same type to that result.

For example:

  • A cannon dealing 1d8 SP deals 2d8 SP.
  • A machine producing a quantity measured in dice adds one die to its output.

Fine Tuning does not add the die to the operator's Check or automatically change a fixed value.

Rolls made to use the machine are modified by abilities such as Machinist, not Fine Tuning.

  • Sabotage
  • Action

You can disable an enemy machine that you can physically reach.

You must have access to a control, mechanism or component that can actually be tampered with.

Make a Cunning + Mechanics Check against the device's Threshold.

If no Threshold is specified:

  • Ordinary machinery → Threshold 8.
  • Complex or protected system → Threshold 12.

If an enemy technician is actively protecting the system, the GM can instead use an opposed Cunning + Mechanics Check.

On a success, the machine becomes Broken and remains unusable until repaired.

Sabotage does not automatically destroy the device.

You cannot sabotage a machine on the other side of a wall or a system to which you have no physical access.

d10 Talents · Pirate

  • Hands in Oil
  • Passive

When you use the Workshop, you complete repairs in half the time. Your tools do not wear out.

  • Machinist
  • Passive

During a naval battle, add your Mechanics Die to the rolls of the machines you operate.

  • Fine Tuning · Improved
  • Prerequisite: Fine Tuning · Basic

With a single Action, you can operate up to two compatible machines.

You must be able to reach their controls or use a station that actually connects them.

Each machine:

  • Retains its own functionality.
  • Makes its rolls separately.
  • Consumes ammunition and energy separately.
  • Receives the compatible benefits of Fine Tuning · Basic as normal.

The Talent does not grant the character two Actions.

  • Tuned Battery
  • Preparation before combat

Before the battle, you overhaul and tune the ship's cannons.

For the entire battle, the Artillery value increases by one step.

At the end of the combat, the cannons must be overhauled before this benefit can be used again.

d12 Talents · Veteran

  • Fine Tuning · Mastery
  • Once per naval battle
  • Prerequisite: Fine Tuning · Improved

Choose a cannon or machine you are operating.

For that round, it can activate twice.

Both activations are resolved separately and consume the device's normal resources.

If the same machine already receives a second activation from the Running Like a Dream Signature or another ability, it does not gain a third activation.

  • Flesh and Metal
  • Passive

When you perform a Field Repair on a Cyborg, add +1 Mechanics Die to the HP recovered.

If the target also has a Damaged Module, you can restore one Module to operational condition during the same repair, provided the materials needed to repair it are available.

Flesh and Metal does not remove any special Module requirements or allow you to reconstruct unique or destroyed components for free.

  • Mechanical Trap
  • Preparation · 5 minutes

Using appropriate tools and materials, you can install a trap at a location, in a passage or on an object.

You can have at most two armed traps at the same time.

Spotting it. A character actively searching for the trap makes Cunning + Observation against Threshold 10.

Disarming it. Requires an Action and Cunning + Mechanics against Threshold 10.

Activation. The first enemy to meet the trap's trigger makes a Technique Save against the Threshold determined by the creator's Mechanics Grade when it was prepared (§1.4). On a success, they avoid the effect; on a failure, they become Off-Balance and Restrained.

Breaking free. The Restrained target can spend an Action and make a Strength or Technique Save against the same trap Threshold. On a success, they break free. This specific procedure takes precedence over the normal Restrained contest.

The trap retains the Threshold determined when it was prepared; a later increase in Mechanics does not retroactively modify traps already prepared.

A triggered trap must be prepared again before it can work once more.

  • Deep Sabotage
  • Prerequisite: Sabotage
  • Action

When you physically reach a Module, a control point or an essential connection on the enemy ship, you can try to disable it completely.

Make Cunning + Mechanics against the system's specified Threshold; if it has none, use Threshold 12.

On a success, the Module remains unusable for the rest of the battle.

The Module is not considered Destroyed: it is deeply sabotaged.

Rapid Response is not enough to restore it to working order during that battle.

An ability that explicitly states it can counter deep sabotage may provide an exception.

  • Gears and Locks
  • Passive · Outside combat

With suitable tools, time and the opportunity to work without interruption, you can open ordinary mechanisms even without their key.

This includes:

  • Padlocks.
  • Handcuffs.
  • Safes.
  • Hatches.
  • Locks and geared mechanisms.

An ordinary mechanism requires up to 10 minutes of work and no Check.

Exceptional, unique or damaged mechanisms, or those built specifically to resist tampering, may require Cunning + Mechanics against their own Threshold.

The Talent applies to genuinely mechanical systems: it does not automatically overcome seals, powers, materials that cannot be worked or systems belonging to a completely different technology.

If the mechanism contains a trap, you spot it before triggering it.

Spotting it does not automatically mean disarming it.

Supreme Talent d20 · Everything at Full Capacity

Everything at Full Capacity

  • Ultimate
  • Cost: 5 ST
  • Frequency: once per naval battle

For one round, you push all the ship's systems beyond their normal operational limits.

During that round:

  • Every cannon and machine actually used can activate twice.
  • Normal ammunition, fuel and energy costs are paid for each activation.
  • Damaged Modules can temporarily work as though intact.

Second activations do not stack with the Signature, Fine Tuning · Mastery or similar effects.

At the end of the round, the overload must cause a breakdown.

The Game Master chooses a system that actually benefited from the Ultimate:

  • An ordinary operational machine becomes Broken.
  • An Intact Module becomes Damaged.
  • An already Damaged Module that was forced to operate may become Destroyed.

The breakdown cannot be arbitrarily assigned to a system that was not used during the overload and does not automatically destroy the entire ship.

Subsequent repairs follow the affected component's normal rules.

Requirements: the Engineer's Ambition and the Game Master's permission.

4.10 · Archaeologist

The Grand Line holds ruins, vanished civilisations, forgotten inscriptions and secrets that many would prefer to leave buried.

The Archaeologist is the specialist who can reconstruct history through what remains of the past. They can interpret ancient documents, discover hidden mechanisms and recognise the significance of apparently meaningless artefacts.

Their knowledge can open a forgotten passage, reveal an enemy weakness or lead the crew to a discovery that changes the course of the adventure.

Role Sheet

CharacteristicValue
RoleArchaeologist
Role SkillArchaeology
Starting Graded8
PathSingle
TraitThe Crew’s Memory
SignatureThe Pinnacle

The Archaeologist uses Archaeology as the reference Skill for accessing their Talents.

Trait · The Crew’s Memory

Free · Always active

The Archaeologist interprets the traces left by past civilisations. Where others see only stones and ruins, they can recognise inscriptions, evidence and information useful to the crew.

The Trait defines their specialisation in historical research. Specific mechanical benefits are granted by acquired Talents.

Signature · The Pinnacle

Free · Always active

When you achieve a successful Archaeology roll with a +4 margin, you can obtain a result beyond what the Check would normally provide.

Depending on the activity:

  • Translation: you fully understand the text and also discover a hidden detail.
  • Exploration: you recognise a trap before it is triggered.

The Signature does not automatically grant both pieces of information: it applies to the activity that produced the result.

Archaeological Examination

When an Archaeologist studies a place, artefact, inscription or material evidence, there must be something from which to derive the information:

  • Architecture.
  • Symbols.
  • Signs of use.
  • Materials.
  • Inscriptions.
  • Damage.
  • Objects.
  • Evidence already known.

An ordinary examination takes approximately 5 minutes.

If the information is uncertain or hidden, the Game Master calls for a Cunning + Archaeology Check against an appropriate Threshold.

On a success, the player can ask a concrete question about the subject examined, and the Game Master provides true, usable information that can be derived from the traces present.

A failure does not provide false information: it means the available traces have not been correctly interpreted or are insufficient to obtain that answer.

A single Check does not make the Archaeologist omniscient or reveal information that has left no trace.

Translation and study times

The time needed depends on the amount and complexity of the material.

MaterialNormal time
Symbol, sentence or short inscription10 minutes
Tablet, stele or medium-length text1 hour
Large inscription, complex code or Poneglyph4 hours
Archive or collectionOne or more study sessions

These times assume the text is physically legible.

Incomplete copies, missing sections or damaged surfaces can make a complete translation impossible.

Talent Tree

Archaeology GradeTier
d8Deckhand
d10Pirate
d12Veteran
d20Legend

Reaching the required Grade allows the corresponding Talents to be acquired through Upgrades. Any prerequisites still apply.

d8 · Deckhand

The initial Talents allow the Archaeologist to study inscriptions, discover hidden passages and exploit the layout of ruins.

Rubbing Master

Passive

You can decipher an inscription even aboard the ship, using a rubbing of it.

Make the Check with Advantage.

The rubbings you create are not consumed during study and can be reused.

Eye of the Ruins

Passive

When examining an ancient site, you can discover traps and hidden passages with an ordinary Success.

The +4 margin required by the Signature is not necessary.

Dead Writing · Basic

Passive

Add +1 die to every Check devoted to translating, deciphering or reading inscriptions.

This benefit applies to interpreting texts and writing, not to every Archaeology Check.

Familiar Ground

Once per combat for cover

Inside a ruin, temple or other ancient site, you ignore penalties for unfamiliar terrain.

Furthermore, once per combat, you can point out an advantageous spot in the surroundings.

Anyone reaching that spot gains Cover.

d10 · Pirate

The Archaeologist deepens their knowledge. They learn to recognise artefacts, interpret lost scripts and derive information from the histories of those they meet.

Antiquarian

Passive

When identifying artefacts or trying to recognise a forgery, you gain an additional die on the Check.

If you successfully reconstruct and reveal an artefact's history, you also increase its value.

The Talent does not specify a fixed monetary increase: the value depends on the object and its context.

Scholar

Passive

Halve the time needed to produce treatises, annotated copies and historical maps.

A historical map you complete can be used by the Navigator as a detailed nautical chart and normally grants +d8 when relevant.

Dead Writing · Improved

Prerequisite: Dead Writing · Basic

You can read scripts that no one else knows any more.

The Talent includes lost alphabets, codes from vanished civilisations and inscriptions normally considered indecipherable.

It does not yet automatically grant the ability to read Poneglyphs, which is acquired with Dead Writing · Mastery.

Insignia and Scars

Recognition

By examining an individual, weapon, emblem, tattoo, uniform or other recognisable signs for at least a moment, you can try to reconstruct part of their history.

Outside combat, the examination normally takes approximately one minute.

During combat it requires your Action.

Make Cunning + Archaeology against the Threshold set by the Game Master.

On a success, you gain true, useful information that can be deduced from the signs observed.

For example, the Talent can reveal:

  • Membership of an organisation.
  • Cultural origin.
  • A war or battle in which the subject seems to have taken part.
  • The meaning of a symbol.
  • The historical function of a weapon or uniform.

It does not automatically reveal thoughts, secret abilities or events that have left no recognisable traces.

d12 · Veteran

The Veteran can interpret exceptionally rare evidence, use the mechanisms of ruins and preserve the crew's discoveries.

Dead Writing · Mastery

Prerequisite: Dead Writing · Improved

You have acquired the ability to read Poneglyphs.

Reading still requires time and effort: having the Talent does not mean instantly understanding their entire contents.

This ability gives the crew direct access to the information held in the ancient inscriptions.

A Poneglyph normally follows the reference time of 4 hours of study for a large inscription.

The Talent allows you to read it: it does not guarantee that a damaged or incomplete surface still contains all the original information.

Ancient Mechanisms

Passive

You can understand and use mechanisms built by ancient civilisations.

When you can examine an intact device for at least 5 minutes, you understand:

  • Its general function.
  • Its normal activation method.
  • Any physically accessible controls.

If the device is intact and accessible, and its operation has been understood, you can use its normal functions without breaking it open and without accidentally triggering traps you have already identified.

Understanding a device does not automatically mean:

  • Finding every hidden trap.
  • Repairing missing parts.
  • Supplying it with energy.
  • Bypassing a deliberately blocked mechanism.
  • Controlling it from a distance when it has no remote control system.

When a mechanism is particularly complex, damaged or must be used under pressure, the Game Master may require Cunning + Archaeology against the device's Threshold.

Ancient Trap

  • Action
  • Prerequisite: Eye of the Ruins

You can use an ancient trap that you have already found and whose operation you understand.

You must be able to reach its control, trigger or another point that can actually be used.

As an Action, you can activate it when a target is already in the zone the trap can affect.

The target resolves the damage, Statuses and Saves specified by the trap itself.

Ancient Trap:

  • Does not create a new trap.
  • Does not change its damage.
  • Does not change its area.
  • Does not allow you to activate it from a distance the device does not support.

The ordinary version does not use a Reaction.

Master of Ancient Mechanisms from the Prestige Manual is what allows you to intervene immediately when a creature reaches the activation point.

Memory of the World

No roll

When the crew hears about a place, ruin, island or artefact, you already know something about it.

The Game Master tells you a true fragment of information, even when it is uncomfortable or unexpected knowledge.

The Talent does not necessarily reveal all the subject's secrets: it guarantees concrete knowledge as a starting point.

Memory of the World does not require an Archaeological Examination or physical traces in front of the character.

It provides historical knowledge the Archaeologist already possesses.

It normally grants one concrete piece of information per subject when that subject first becomes relevant in the adventure.

Hearing the same name repeatedly does not automatically produce new information.

Keeper of Knowledge

Passive

The knowledge you have gathered is preserved through archives, rubbings and copies also kept elsewhere.

If the ship's library is destroyed or the original documents are lost, the crew retains access to the results of your studies.

The Talent protects knowledge already acquired, not information you have yet to discover.

d20 · Legend

Supreme Talent · The Voice of Stones

The Voice of Stones

  • Ultimate
  • Once per adventure

The Archaeologist gains an extraordinary understanding of the evidence and mechanisms left by ancient civilisations.

The Talent can be used in two different ways.

Outside combat · Revelation

By studying an inscription or artefact, you derive concrete, usable information that changes the course of the story arc.

The discovery is provided by the Game Master and must have a real consequence for the adventure.

Inside a ruin · Dominion over mechanisms

For one scene, you completely understand the control system of the ancient mechanisms present at the site.

You can command only devices that:

  • Actually exist.
  • Still work.
  • Are connected to the system you have understood.
  • Have the energy or force they need to function.

The ability does not reconstruct missing parts or create functions the device does not possess.

Opening a door can only open that door. A counterweight system can move only the parts actually connected to it. A trap uses its own damage, area and Saves.

The Ultimate does not grant additional Actions.

Activating a mechanism uses the Action normally specified by the device; if the device has no specific procedure, issuing a meaningful command requires your Action.

You can control a mechanism remotely only if the ruin actually has a connection that allows it.

The narrative revelation of the Ultimate's other form instead remains in the Game Master's hands and must produce a concrete consequence for the adventure.

Requirements: the Archaeologist's Ambition and the Game Master's permission.

The Voice of Stones can be used only once per adventure, choosing one of its two applications when activated.

4.11 · Musician

The Musician is the crew's voice. Their melodies can restore energy to companions, break fear, weaken opponents and turn a disorganised crew into a group capable of acting in unison.

Their specialisation is not limited to combat. A song can open the doors of a hostile port, pass on a secret message or make a crew famous before it even arrives.

Role Sheet

CharacteristicValue
RoleMusician
Role SkillArt
Starting Graded8
PathSingle
TraitThe Crew’s Force Multiplier
SignatureThe Note That Reaches the Soul

The Musician uses Art as the reference Skill for accessing their Talents.

Trait · The Crew’s Force Multiplier

Free · Always active

The Musician supports the crew through their Songs. They can restore Stamina during battle, counter fear and grant benefits to companions.

A musical instrument is not automatically a weapon. To fight directly, the Musician uses the normal combat rules and the abilities they can access.

Signature · The Note That Reaches the Soul

Free · Always active

Outside combat, when you achieve a successful Art roll with a +4 margin, your performance wins over its listeners.

You can obtain something the audience did not initially intend to grant you, such as a favour, information, passage or a promise to keep silent about an event.

The concession must be consistent with the situation and the people involved.

The Signature does not activate on Songs in battle: these do not require a roll from the Musician and therefore do not produce a +4 margin.

How Songs work

Songs are a special category of Musician Techniques.

The Musician does not make a roll to play a Song. They spend their Action and the Stamina required by the Melody, then the effect is produced.

RecipientResolution
AllyAutomatically receives the effect without making any rolls.
EnemyMakes a Save when the Melody requires one.
Save ThresholdDetermined by the musical instrument's Grade.

Each Melody constitutes a complete Song: a Technique can contain only one Melody.

Songs requiring upkeep follow their own duration and cost rules. The Counterpoint Talents progressively modify these limits.

Talent Tree

Art GradeTier
d8Deckhand
d10Pirate
d12Veteran
d20Legend

Talents must be acquired through Upgrades. Reaching a new Art Grade makes them available, but does not automatically grant them.

d8 · Deckhand

Unwavering Music

Passive

Your music does not stop when you become Stunned or are knocked down.

You can therefore continue to sustain a Song despite these conditions.

The Talent does not grant immunity to Statuses: it prevents them from being sufficient, on their own, to stop your performance.

Entertainer’s Soul

Passive

Outside combat, you make your performances with Advantage.

The required roll is:

Spirit + Art, with Advantage.

This ability applies to artistic performances, not to every Check using Spirit.

Counterpoint · Basic

Passive

Sustaining a Song no longer prevents you from acting.

You can continue playing and take your turn as normal.

The Talent does not remove the Song's upkeep costs: it only changes your ability to act while sustaining it.

Battle Rhythm

Passive · Additional benefit once per combat

At the start of combat, every ally who can hear you gains:

+1 to Initiative.

Furthermore, once per combat, you can have two companions act consecutively, temporarily skipping the characters who would be between them in Initiative order.

This benefit changes the order of actions, but does not grant the companions an extra turn.

d10 · Pirate

Long Breath

Passive

Your Songs' cost decreases by 1 ST, to a minimum of 1 ST.

The reduction cannot make a Song that normally requires Stamina free.

Counterpoint · Improved

Passive

Prerequisite: Counterpoint · Basic.

You can sustain two active Songs at the same time.

The Songs retain their respective effects and costs. This Talent increases the number of Songs you can sustain without removing the Stamina required.

Requiem

Passive · Enhances Mocking Requiem

You have perfected the Mocking Requiem Melody.

Mocking Requiem retains:

  • Minimum Grade d8.
  • Cost 1 ST.
  • The normal Action required to perform a Song.
  • One target who can hear you.
  • The normal Save determined by the instrument.

If the target fails the Save, they suffer Disadvantage on their next roll.

If the target has already lost at least 1 HP during combat, they make the Save against Mocking Requiem with Disadvantage.

Requiem is not a second Melody and cannot be used at the same time as a separate copy of Mocking Requiem.

The Song That Remains

Passive

You can hide a message in a melody: a course, a name, a combination or another piece of information.

Anyone who knows the tune remembers the message perfectly, even many years later.

Those who do not know it hear only an ordinary song and do not recognise the hidden content.

d12 · Veteran

Counterpoint · Mastery

Passive

Prerequisite: Counterpoint · Improved.

Sustaining your Songs no longer requires Stamina per turn.

You pay only the cost needed to perform them.

The Songs' other requirements still apply, including the maximum of two sustained simultaneously thanks to Counterpoint · Improved.

Crew Chorus

Passive

When at least two companions actually join you in singing, your Songs' reach is doubled.

Furthermore, aboard a ship, the Song can spread through every connected space where the Chorus can actually be heard.

The two companions joining the Chorus:

  • Must be conscious.
  • Must be able to vocalise or contribute musically.
  • Must voluntarily choose to take part.
  • Do not spend an Action, Bonus Action or Movement merely to support the Chorus.
  • Can continue fighting and acting as normal.

If fewer than two companions can continue to take part, the Chorus's benefit ends immediately.

Crew Chorus does not make the ship an ordinary target of Songs.

A Melody that affects people continues to affect people.

Only a Melody explicitly stating that it affects a ship, station or naval activity — such as Shipboard Song — produces that effect.

Funeral March

Offensive Melody · Sustained Song

Prerequisite: Requiem.

Action · 3 ST + 1 ST per turn · Radius: 10 metres

Perform a melody capable of breaking the enemies' courage.

When the Song is performed, every enemy within 10 metres who can hear you makes a Spirit Save against the Song's Threshold.

Those who fail become Terrified while:

  • They remain within the Song's reach.
  • They can still hear you.
  • You continue to sustain Funeral March.

A target who leaves its reach or can no longer hear the music immediately ceases to be Terrified.

Funeral March is a Sustained Song and follows all the normal upkeep rules.

Playing for the Fallen

1 time per battle

When a companion who is still alive reaches 0 HP and can hear you, you can immediately begin supporting them through your music.

The companion remains conscious and Stabilised at 0 HP while you continue playing.

During this period, they can:

  • Use their Movement as normal.
  • Use an Action.
  • Use a Bonus Action.
  • Use a Reaction.
  • Use Techniques and Talents normally available to them.

They nevertheless remain at 0 HP: the Talent is not healing.

If the companion takes further damage while still at 0 HP, Playing for the Fallen immediately ends for them and the normal consequences for a Downed character who continues to take damage apply.

If they receive actual healing and rise above 0 HP, they no longer need the support and continue normally with their recovered HP.

If the music stops while the companion still has 0 HP, they immediately lose consciousness and return to following the normal Downed rules.

To keep supporting them, you must be able to produce music and the companion must continue to hear you.

Playing for the Fallen is not Field Treatment and does not consume its limit.

Legend in Music

Passive

Your songs spread from port to port, contributing to the crew's fame even in places it has not yet visited.

You gain Advantage on Checks to make yourself heard, recognised or believed before crowds and crews.

The Talent represents the reputation built through your music, not automatic control over listeners' decisions.

d20 · Legend

Supreme Talent · Song of the Soul

Song of the Soul

  • Ultimate
  • Activation: Action
  • Cost: 6 ST
  • Frequency: 1 time per scene
  • d20 Signature Melody from the Melody Catalogue

The Musician performs a Melody capable of calling forth the entire crew's energy.

Every member of your crew who can hear you, including the Musician, immediately restores their Stamina to its current maximum value.

The cost of 6 ST is paid before applying recovery.

Song of the Soul does not recover Haki PIP, HP or other resources.

The same crew can benefit from Song of the Soul only once per scene, regardless of how many Musicians it contains.

A second Musician therefore cannot use their own Song of the Soul in the same scene to restore the same crew's Stamina again.

Requirements: the Musician's Ambition and the Game Master's permission.

4.12 · Captain

The Captain holds the crew together and directs its actions. During combat, they coordinate allies, identify priority targets and issue orders that can change the course of the battle.

Outside combat, they represent the crew in negotiations, form alliances and make decisions that determine its course.

Role Sheet

CharacteristicValue
RoleCaptain
Role SkillCommunication
Starting Graded8
PathSingle
TraitThe Commanding Voice
SignatureThe Order That Changes Everything

Trait · The Commanding Voice

Free · Always active

The Captain is the only Role that can directly turn Communication into Orders that make a companion move and act.

Outside combat, Communication also represents their ability to handle negotiations, agreements, threats and alliances between crews.

The specific effects of Orders, including costs and frequency, are defined by their respective Talents.

Signature · The Order That Changes Everything

Free · Always active · At most once per round

The Signature triggers when the Captain achieves:

  • A Clean Hit with a +4 margin, or
  • A successful Communication Check with a +4 margin.

Choose an ally who can see or hear you.

The ally can immediately take an Action outside their own turn.

They do not receive Movement, a Bonus Action or a new Reaction.

The granted Action does not consume the ally's normal turn.

The ally pays ST, PIP, ammunition and any other resource required by the chosen Action as normal.

The Signature can trigger at most once per round, regardless of how many +4 margins you achieve.

The granted Action follows the normal rules for out-of-turn Actions and cannot generate another chain of Actions through Orders, this same Signature or equivalent abilities.

Talent Tree

CommunicationTier
d8Deckhand
d10Pirate
d12Veteran
d20Legend

Every Talent must be acquired through Upgrades. Reaching the required Grade allows its acquisition, but does not automatically grant it.

d8 · Deckhand

War Cry

Free · Once per combat

The Captain inspires their companions with a cry that reaches every ally able to hear it.

Each affected ally:

  • Removes one removable negative Condition of their choice.
  • Adds +1 die to their next roll.

War Cry does not remove physical or environmental causes that continue to apply a Status.

The Talent does not require Stamina.

Captain’s Order · Basic

Action · 2 ST · Once per combat

Choose an ally within 10 metres who can clearly perceive your Order.

The Order can be issued verbally or through an unambiguous gesture, provided the ally can actually hear or see you.

The ally immediately takes an Action outside their own turn.

They do not receive Movement, a Bonus Action or an additional Reaction.

The Captain spends their Action to issue the Order.

The ally retains their normal turn and pays all costs of the chosen Action.

The granted Action follows the normal rules for out-of-turn Actions.

A Den Den Mushi or another means of communication can transmit an ordinary message, but does not automatically extend an Order's mechanical reach.

An ability or piece of equipment must explicitly state it can transmit Orders beyond their normal reach.

Commander’s Eye

Passive

At the start of combat, the Captain and their allies can freely swap positions in Initiative order.

After establishing the order, the Captain can designate a Priority Target.

Only one Priority Target can exist at a time.

The first ally to attack that target during each round treats its Passive Defence as reduced by 2 for that attack.

At the start of each new round, before the first character acts, the Captain can change the Priority Target for free.

If they do not declare a new target, the previous one remains.

If the target is defeated, the Captain can designate a new one at the start of the following round.

The benefit can be used only once per round, even if several allies attack the same target.

Example: the Captain identifies an officer with Passive Defence 13 and declares them the priority target. The first ally to attack them in the round treats their Passive Defence as 11.

Crew Shield

Reaction · Target within 5 metres

Requirement: Nakama Bond of at least d8.

When an ally within 5 metres is about to take damage from a source you can physically protect them against, you can use your Reaction to interpose yourself and take that damage in their place.

For example, Crew Shield can intercept:

  • A direct attack.
  • A projectile.
  • A melee strike.
  • A Technique that directly deals damage.
  • The portion of damage the ally takes from an Area effect, if you can realistically place your body in its way.

It cannot transfer ongoing damage or damage without an interceptable source, such as:

  • Poisoning already applied.
  • Burning already active.
  • Suffocation damage.
  • A fall.
  • Self-inflicted damage.
  • Other consequences that cannot be physically intercepted.

Once the damage has been transferred, the ally does not take it.

The Captain reduces the transferred Damage Dice by one die, to a minimum of one die.

They then apply their normal passive protections, Damage Reductions and other compatible defensive effects.

The ally's defences are not applied, since they are no longer the one receiving the damage.

Since Crew Shield uses the Captain's Reaction, the Captain cannot use a second ability that requires a Reaction against the same damage.

If the Captain was already independently affected by the same Area effect, they resolve their own damage and the ally's transferred damage separately.

When the Nakama Bond reaches d20, instead of reducing the Damage Dice by one, halve the transferred damage after the roll, rounding down.

The Captain's normal passive protections are applied afterwards.

d10 · Pirate

Captain’s Order · Improved

Prerequisite: Captain’s Order · Basic.

The usage limit of Captain’s Order increases to twice per combat.

Each Order retains:

  • The Captain's Action.
  • The cost of 2 ST.
  • The normal reach of 10 metres.
  • All the Order's normal rules.

The new limit replaces that of the Basic version.

A Voice That Carries Far

Passive

The reach of your Orders and your War Cry is no longer limited to the usual 10 metres.

During combat, they reach every ally on the battlefield who can actually see or hear you.

During a naval battle, they can reach your entire ship.

The Talent changes only reach. Costs, Actions, frequencies and other requirements remain unchanged.

The Plan

Preparation before combat

If you have had the opportunity to prepare for combat and explain a plan to the crew, create a shared pool of:

3 rerolls

If Communication is d12 or higher:

4 rerolls

A reroll allows an ally to reroll a single die just rolled.

The decision is made after seeing the die's result, but before the roll's consequences are finally applied.

The new result replaces the previous one, even if it is worse.

The same die cannot be rerolled more than once.

Multiple rerolls can be spent on the same pool, provided each is used on a different die.

The reserve belongs to the crew as a whole. When a reroll is used, it is removed from the reserve.

Parley

Action · At most 1 attempt per target per combat

During combat, you can try to impose a brief truce to speak with an opponent able to hear and understand you.

Ordinary enemy: listens automatically. Important enemy: make an opposed Check:
  • Captain: Spirit + Communication.
  • Opponent: Spirit.

On a tie, the opponent prevails.

If the Captain succeeds, on the opponent's next turn they must devote their Action to listening and responding to the negotiation instead of taking a hostile Action.

They retain Movement, Bonus Action and Reaction when these can be used non-hostilely or defensively.

After they have listened, the Captain can make the normal Communication Check required by the proposal: a truce, information, surrender, a duel, an exchange or another appropriate agreement.

Parley only compels them to listen. It does not compel them to accept the proposal.

The effect ends immediately if:

  • The Captain or one of their allies attacks the target.
  • The target is deliberately subjected to another hostile action by the crew.
  • The situation makes continuing the dialogue physically impossible.

If the effect ends before the opponent's turn, they act as normal.

Regardless of the result, Parley cannot be attempted again against the same target during that combat.

d12 · Veteran

Captain’s Order · Mastery

Prerequisite: Captain’s Order · Improved.

You still have 2 Orders per combat.

When an ally takes the Action granted by one of your Orders, they make any required roll with Advantage.

The cost remains 2 ST, and issuing the Order uses your Action.

Reach and the rules for out-of-turn Actions apply as normal.

Second in Command

1 time per battle

At the start of combat or during your turn, appoint a companion as your Deputy.

Once during that combat, the Deputy can use their Action to issue one of your ordinary Captain’s Orders.

The Deputy:

  • Personally pays the required 2 ST.
  • Uses the Order's normal reach.
  • Must be seen or heard by the ally receiving the Order.
  • Consumes one of the Captain’s Order uses still available in your reserve.

If you have Captain’s Order · Mastery, the Action granted by the Deputy receives Advantage as normal.

Second in Command does not grant the Deputy the Captain's other Talents or allow them to use Sovereign Order from the Prestige Manual.

If the Captain falls, the Deputy retains this option until the end of combat.

No One Left Behind

Reaction · 1 time per combat

Prerequisite: Crew Shield.

When a companion with whom you have a Nakama Bond reaches 0 HP, you can use your Reaction to stop them from collapsing.

The companion remains conscious and is temporarily considered to have 1 HP until the end of their next turn.

This HP represents only the character's ability to keep acting thanks to the Captain's order and is not healing.

During this window, the companion can use Movement, Action, Bonus Action and Reaction as normal.

If they take at least 1 damage, the support ends immediately: they return to 0 HP and follow the normal Downed rules.

If they actually recover at least 1 HP through healing or another ability before the effect ends, No One Left Behind ends and the character keeps the HP actually recovered as normal.

If they receive no healing, the support ends at the end of their next turn and the character returns to 0 HP, becoming Downed under the normal rules.

The temporary HP cannot be increased or recovered: any actual healing directly replaces this temporary condition.

Orderly Retreat

Action

The Captain orders the crew to disengage from combat.

All companions can immediately move their Speed without provoking Reaction attacks caused by disengaging.

The Talent allows a coordinated retreat. It does not prevent enemies from pursuing the crew on subsequent turns.

d20 · Legend

Supreme Talent · All Hands, Board!

All Hands, Board!

  • Ultimate
  • 5 ST · once per scene

The Captain issues an order that immediately mobilises the entire crew.

Every ally who can see or hear them acts immediately, outside their own turn.

Each affected ally gains:

  • Movement
  • Action

Once this sequence is over, combat resumes as normal in the previous Initiative order.

Requirements: the Captain's Ambition and the Game Master's permission.

All Hands, Board! grants each affected ally immediate Movement and an Action. It does not replace the character's normal turn or permanently change Initiative.

Chapter 5 ·

Skills and Checks

A pirate may be strong enough to lift an anchor, yet not know how to repair it. They may have excellent aim and fail to recognise a trap hidden beneath their feet.

Attributes represent the character's fundamental abilities. Skills indicate what they have learnt to do through experience, study and training.

When an action's outcome is uncertain, Attributes and Skills work together to determine the result.

5.1 · How to make a Check

A Check is called for when a character attempts an action that could fail and whose outcome matters to the scene.

The player declares what they want to achieve. The Game Master identifies the appropriate Attribute and Skill, sets a Threshold and calls for the roll.

The basic Check

Attribute + Skill

Roll both dice and add their results together.

Total ≥ Threshold: the action succeeds.

Total below the Threshold: the action fails.

Example: a pirate wants to spot a hidden sentry. The GM calls for Cunning + Observation, with Threshold 8.

The character has Cunning d8 and Observation d6. They roll 6 and 4, for a total of 10.

The Check succeeds.

There is no need to roll for every activity. If the character can perform an action without significant risks or obstacles, the GM directly describes the result.

5.2 · Thresholds

The Threshold represents the difficulty to reach. The GM sets it based on the situation before resolving the Check.

ThresholdDifficulty
4Trivial
8Normal
12Expert
16Vice Admiral level
20Admiral level
25+Legendary

The Threshold depends not only on the declared action, but also on the circumstances.

Climbing a dry wall with plenty of handholds is not necessarily as difficult as scaling a wet ship's side during a storm.

The GM can therefore use different Thresholds for apparently similar actions.

A tie is enough to succeed: if the Threshold is 12 and the total result is 12, the Check succeeds.

5.3 · Margin of success

The margin measures how far the result exceeds the Threshold.

Margin = total rolled − Threshold

A result that just meets the requirement achieves the objective. A high margin may produce a particularly effective success.

MarginInterpretation
0Barely successful
+1 / +3Success
+4 or higherSuccess with a high margin

The GM can use the margin to enrich the narrative, granting information or benefits consistent with the action.

The +4 margin also has a specific importance: it can trigger Role Signatures and other effects that explicitly require it.

For example, a Navigator who succeeds on a Navigation Check with a +4 margin triggers Perfect Course.

A +4 margin does not, however, automatically grant a universal bonus to every Check. Follow the effect of the ability involved.

5.4 · The sixteen Skills

There are sixteen Skills in Grand Line Chronicles. Each represents a distinct discipline.

SkillUses
AcrobaticsBalance, tumbling, evasion.
ArchaeologyHistory, ancient languages, inscriptions and Poneglyphs.
ArtMusic, drawing, performances and artistic forgeries.
CraftingMaking objects and tools.
AthleticsRunning, climbing, wrestling and physical exertion.
CarpentryBuilding and repairing the ship.
CommunicationPersuading, negotiating, commanding and intimidating.
CookingPreparing meals and recovery through food.
ExplorationFinding your way and searching while exploring islands.
StealthMoving unseen and picking locks.
MechanicsMachinery, cannons and devices.
MedicineWounds, poisons, treatment and stabilisation.
NatureAnimals, plants and weather phenomena.
NavigationLog Poses, courses, currents and crossings.
ObservationDangers, clues and intentions.
SurvivalObtaining food, water and shelter.

Skills are not interchangeable merely because they can contribute to the same objective.

Example: to reach a hidden place, a pirate might use Exploration to find the route, Athletics to scale a wall and Stealth to avoid the guards.

These are three different activities, even though they belong to the same plan.

5.5 · Which Attribute should you use?

The GM chooses the Attribute consistent with the declared action. Skills are not permanently tied to a single Attribute.

A character trying to climb might use Strength + Athletics. To spot a hidden clue, they might use Cunning + Observation.

The choice should depend on how the character approaches the situation.

There is no need to invent a new Skill to represent a different approach. Use the sixteen existing disciplines, paired with the appropriate Attribute.

5.6 · Skills at creation

Each character starts with five developed Skills.

AllocationQuantity
Role Skill at d81
Free-choice Skills at d62
Free-choice Skills at d42

The other eleven Skills start at 0.

The Role Skill is determined by the chosen Role or Style. The four additional Skills are freely selected.

If the character has two Roles, they still receive only one starting d8 as a Role Skill: the player chooses which of the two receives it.

Skills at 0

A Skill at 0 represents a discipline the character has not yet developed.

The first time they use or train it meaningfully, with the Game Master's approval, it can unlock directly at d4.

From then on, it can be improved by spending Ability Points.

Unlocking must come from a concrete experience. Simply declaring that every Skill has been used once is not enough to immediately gain sixteen proficiencies.

5.7 · Bonus dice and unfavourable conditions

Circumstances can modify a Check.

A favourable position, careful preparation, a companion's help or a special effect may grant bonus dice to add to the roll.

Adverse conditions may instead increase the Threshold or subtract dice, according to the applicable effect.

Example: a character tries to repair a device. If they have appropriate tools and benefit from an ability that adds Mechanics to the roll, they add the indicated die to the normal Check.

Benefits explicitly granting Advantage or Disadvantage follow their respective general rules.

Do not add dice or modifiers merely because an action is described spectacularly: mechanical benefits come from conditions and abilities that actually apply.

5.8 · Skills and Techniques

Skills and Techniques represent two different elements of the character sheet.

A Skill is a general discipline used in many situations: Medicine to heal, Communication to negotiate, Athletics to climb.

A Technique is a specific move or power, with its own die and any particular effects.

For an ordinary Check, use:

Attribute + Skill.

When the situation calls for a Technique, follow that Technique's specific rules instead of automatically replacing it with a Skill.

Skills improve through Ability Points. Racial and Custom Techniques neither accumulate nor spend AP: racial Techniques follow developments granted by the GM, while the limit of Custom Techniques depends on their associated Attribute.

Chapter 6 ·

Combat

On the Grand Line, combat can begin with a cannon shot, a punch or one provocation too many. When it does, every combatant must decide how to use their turn: attack immediately, prepare a Technique, activate Haki or wait for the right moment to unleash a Special Move.

Combat in Grand Line Chronicles is based on four fundamental resources: Movement, Action, Bonus Action and Reaction.

Techniques determine what a character can perform. Talents expand their options. Haki can enhance their abilities, but requires careful resource management.

6.1 · Initiative

At the start of every combat, all combatants make an Initiative roll.

Initiative roll

Technique + Cunning

Combatants act from the highest result to the lowest.

Initiative order is kept in subsequent rounds unless an ability changes it.

Example: three combatants roll 16, 12 and 8 respectively. The first acts, then the second and finally the third. Once their turns end, a new round begins.

6.2 · The combat turn

Each character normally has the following resources.

ResourceQuantityFunction
Movement1Move within your Speed.
Action1Attack, use a Technique or perform a Special Move.
Bonus Action1Activate an active Talent or Haki.
Reaction1Respond to an event that allows a reaction.

The character can use Movement, Action and Bonus Action in any order they prefer, observing the requirements of the abilities used.

Movement can also be split before and after the Action.

An Action cannot freely be converted into a second Bonus Action, nor can two Bonus Actions be used in the same turn.

A Talent or ability explicitly granting an additional Action is an exception and follows its own description.

6.2.1 · The Action

The Action represents the character's main activity.

It can be used to make a basic attack, perform a Technique, use a Special Move or carry out an activity requiring an Action.

Additional attacks can only be made when an ability explicitly grants them.

For example, Flurry allows extra attacks within the Talent's limits and costs. These attacks are not new main Actions.

6.2.2 · The Bonus Action

The Bonus Action allows you to activate an ability without consuming the main Action.

Each turn, you can perform only one of the following activations:

Active Talent

Activate a Talent and pay any Stamina cost.

Haki

Activate Haki and pay its activation cost.

Fundamental rule

You can activate only one active Talent per turn.

If you use the Bonus Action to activate Haki, you cannot activate an active Talent in the same turn.

If you use it for an active Talent, you cannot activate Haki in the same turn.

A Talent listing a cost of 0 ST does not automatically become passive: its classification matters. If it requires activation and is an active Talent, it still uses the Bonus Action.

Passive Talents

Passive Talents apply automatically when the described conditions are met.

They do not consume Bonus Actions or require voluntary activation.

A passive Talent does not bypass its own limits, however: if it can apply only once per turn or per combat, that limit still applies.

Abilities without an explicit classification

If an ability requires the player to choose voluntarily whether to apply or activate it when its conditions are met, it is considered an active ability.

If no other resource is specified, activation uses the Bonus Action.

If instead the effect applies automatically when its specified conditions are met, it is considered passive and does not use the Bonus Action.

An ability explicitly specifying an Action, Reaction or another activation method naturally uses that specific rule.

Reaction Talents

Talents triggered in response to a specific event and requiring a Reaction use the Reaction, not the Bonus Action.

For example, Crew Shield is used when an ally is about to take damage.

6.2.3 · Actions granted outside the turn

Some abilities allow a character to act immediately outside their own turn.

When an ability simply grants an Action, the character receives only an ordinary Action.

They do not automatically receive:

  • Movement.
  • A Bonus Action.
  • An additional Reaction.
  • A new turn.

Resources already consumed are not recovered, and the abilities used retain their costs and limits.

The granted Action can trigger Signatures, passive Talents and other compatible effects as normal.

However, an Action gained outside the turn cannot be used to activate another ability whose main effect is itself to grant an Action, Movement or a turn outside the normal Initiative order.

This restriction prevents Orders, Signatures or similar abilities from generating chains of Actions between multiple characters.

When an ability explicitly grants Movement, a Bonus Action or other resources too, its specific description takes precedence.

6.3 · Haki activation and upkeep

Haki requires activation before benefits dependent on its active state can apply.

Activating Haki

1 Bonus ActionSpecified activation cost

Activation consumes the turn's Bonus Action.

Once active, Haki can be used under the rules of its Colour and effects.

Upkeep

Haki already active does not need to be activated again each turn.

To sustain it, the character pays the specified upkeep cost. For abilities requiring 1 ST per turn, they therefore pay 1 ST during each turn in which upkeep continues.

Upkeep does not consume a new Bonus Action unless a specific effect states otherwise.

This allows Haki to be activated on one turn and the next turn's Bonus Action to be used for an active Talent.

Example: a fighter activates Armament Haki on their first turn. On their second, they sustain it by paying the required ST and use their Bonus Action to activate Flurry.

If Haki is no longer active, it must be activated again before its benefits apply.

6.4 · Special Moves

A Special Move is a customised combination of the character's own abilities.

It can include a Technique, Builder effects, Haki benefits and compatible Talents.

The Special Move collects these components into a move ready to use and consult during combat.

Special Moves rule

A Special Move does not grant additional effects, remove costs or change the Action economy.

All its components must be usable when the move is performed.

Passive effects apply automatically when their conditions are met.

Haki must be active.

The Special Move combines an ordinary Action, compatible passive effects and at most one effect activated with the Bonus Action: an active Talent or an active Haki effect (⚡). The first activation of a Colour also requires the Bonus Action: these activations cannot be combined in the same turn.

An offensive Special Move normally uses the main Action to make the attack.

Its preparation may instead require one or more turns, depending on the abilities involved.

6.4.1 · Activations in the same turn

If a Special Move requires Haki, but does not require activation of an active Talent or an active Haki effect (⚡), the character can:

  • Activate Haki with the Bonus Action.
  • Perform the Special Move with the Action.

The two operations can happen in the same turn because they use different resources.

6.4.2 · Activations across several turns

If a Special Move requires both Haki activation and an active Talent, the two activations cannot take place in the same turn.

The character can prepare the combination by activating Haki on the first turn, sustaining it on the second and then using the Bonus Action for the Talent.

The combination works only if every required effect still applies when it is performed.

Instantaneous Talents or Haki effects cannot be accumulated in advance. An effect prepared on an earlier turn remains available only if its duration allows this and it has not yet expired. A cost already paid is not repeated, but the Special Move neither renews nor extends the duration.

6.4.3 · Complete example: Diable Jambe, Burning Combo

A fighter has a Special Move that uses Armament Haki and the Flurry · Mastery Talent.

The move also contains passive effects that apply when their respective conditions are met.

Burning Combo

Preparation and execution sequence

Turn 1 · Preparation

Haki

Bonus Action: activate Armament Haki, paying its activation cost.

Action: you can still make an ordinary attack or take another available action.

Haki remains active according to its duration and upkeep rules.

Turn 2 · Execution

Combo

Upkeep: pay the specified cost to keep Haki active.

Action: perform Burning Combo with the initial unarmed attack.

After the initial attack, Bonus Action: activate Flurry · Mastery.

You can attempt up to three extra unarmed basic attacks, paying 1 ST, 2 ST and 3 ST respectively before each: the complete sequence costs 6 ST, in addition to the initial attack and Haki costs. You can stop after any extra attack; a miss does not interrupt the sequence. Ordinary extra attacks do not reuse the Burning Combo Technique.

Passive effects apply automatically when their conditions are met.

If the character hits the same target at least twice during the turn, Constant Pressure can trigger, provided it is among their passive Talents.

There is no need to spend a second Bonus Action to apply it.

The Special Move does not bring forward or grant any free activation: it describes a combination the character must prepare and perform while observing the rules.

6.5 · Movement and distances

Distances are expressed in metres.

There is no need to use a grid: the Game Master can describe positions narratively and use measurements when needed to determine reach, cover or Displacement possibilities.

DistanceReference
1–2 mClose combat
5 mNearby ally
10 mA room or part of the deck
20 mA significant shooting distance
50 m and beyondLarge areas and landscape scale

Racial Movement

RaceMovement
Human10 m
Fish-Man / Merman10 m
Fish-Man / Merman, in water20 m
Giant14 m
Mink14 m
Mink, in Sulong16 m
Lunarian10 m
Cyborg10 m
Tontatta8 m

There is no Run Action. Forgoing your Action does not double Movement.

The character can exceed their Speed only when a specific ability permits it.

Climbing and swimming

Climbing and swimming require double Movement.

A character with Movement 10 metres can therefore climb 5 metres.

A Fish-Man does not suffer this penalty when moving in water.

Jumping and falling

With a short run-up, a character can make a long jump of up to half their Movement.

Falls begin to cause damage from a height of 3 metres. The GM determines the consequences based on the distance and situation.

6.6 · Melee reach

Reach indicates the maximum distance at which a character can hit a target in melee.

Attacker or weaponReach
Unarmed and short weapons1 m
Spears, staves and other long weapons2 m
Giants and large Zoans2–5 m

The reach specified by a particular weapon or Technique takes precedence over the general values.

Moving away from an enemy

When a character leaves the reach of an opponent threatening them, that opponent can use their Reaction to make an attack.

Some Talents allow disengaging without provoking Reaction attacks.

An Off-Balance character cannot make this attack if the Status prevents them from using Reactions.

Forced Displacement

Displacement caused by Push, Launch, Attraction, Drag or another ability that forcibly moves a creature does not provoke Reaction attacks for moving away.

If forced Displacement would take a creature over an edge, off a surface or into a position where they could reasonably try to grab hold, the target can make a Strength Save.

If they succeed on the Save, they manage to grab hold or stop the fall when physically possible.

The Game Master sets the Threshold based on the situation.

6.7 · Attacking

When a character makes an attack, they use an Attribute and the die appropriate to the attack type.

Basic weapon attack

Attribute + Weapon Grade

Damage: Weapon Die.

Basic unarmed attack

Attribute + Role Skill used for combat

Damage: die of the same Role Skill.

Technique attack

Attribute + Technique Die

Damage: Technique Die, unless modified.

The Attribute and Skill used must be compatible with how the character is fighting.

The Attribute used is the one specified by the weapon or Technique.

The result is compared with the target's Passive Defence, unless they use a defence permitted by their abilities.

6.8 · Passive Defence

Passive Defence (PD) represents the ability to avoid or withstand an attack without making a defensive roll.

Passive Defence and Prestige

When the Racial Attribute enters Prestige, Passive Defence continues to depend on its complete Grade. To determine it, add the maximum values of all dice making up the Racial Attribute's Grade, then add +1. No roll is made.

Passive Defence = sum of the maximum values of the Racial Attribute Grade + 1.

Racial AttributePassive Defence
d45
d67
d89
d1011
d1213
d2021
d20+d4 · Prestige I25
d20+d6 · Prestige II27
d20+d8 · Prestige III29
d20+d10 · Prestige IV31
d20+d12 · Prestige V33
d20+d20 · Saikyō41

Explicit Passive Defence modifiers granted by Talents, Techniques, cover, Haki, Unique Traits or other effects apply after determining this base value.

Example: a character with a d20+d8 Racial Attribute has PD 29. With Monstrous Body, PD becomes 31; with Living Fortress, it becomes 33. Living Fortress replaces Monstrous Body's +2 bonus with +4 total: the two values are not added together.

6.9 · Attack margin

To determine an attack's outcome, calculate its margin:

Margin = attack result − PD

MarginResult
Below 0Miss
0Successful defence, no damage
+1 / +3Hit
+4 or higherClean Hit

If the target chose Active Defence, the attack Margin is calculated against the Active Defence result instead of Passive Defence.

A tie is considered a successful defence and cannot produce a Hit or Clean Hit.

Unlike ordinary Checks, matching Passive Defence is not enough to deal damage.

Clean Hit

An attack with a margin of at least +4 is a Clean Hit.

The character deals normal damage and applies any abilities triggered by this result, including compatible Signatures.

A Clean Hit does not automatically double damage.

Example: a pirate attacks an enemy with PD 9 and rolls 14.

The margin is +5: the attack is a Clean Hit.

6.10 · Active Defence and Reactions

Active Defence uses the character's Reaction. It must be declared when they are chosen as a target, before the attacker's attack roll; it replaces Passive Defence for that attack and does not allow a return to it after a failure.

Attack Technique as Active Defence

Besides Dodge and Block, you can use a compatible attack Technique as Active Defence. Declare this choice when selected as a target, before the attacker's attack roll. Spend the Reaction and immediately pay all the Technique's normal ST costs; failure does not refund Stamina. Make the normal Technique roll: Attribute + Technique Die, unless specific rules apply. If you meet or exceed the attacker's roll, you neutralise the attack; ties favour the defender. A Technique used to defend does not normally deal damage or automatically apply its offensive effects. With the Counterblow Talent, only if the defence exceeds the opponent's roll do you also deal the Technique's normal Damage Die. A tie defends but deals no damage.

Parry

A character wielding a compatible weapon can attempt an ordinary Parry, spending their Reaction and observing any costs and requirements of the ability used. This does not require a Parry effect in the Builder.

Attribute + Weapon Die

The Parry succeeds if it meets or exceeds the enemy's attack roll. A successful Parry avoids the hit, but does not automatically deal damage. Counterblow instead requires Active Defence performed with a compatible attacking Technique.

Reactions are independent of the Bonus Action: a character who activated Haki or a Talent on their turn can still react if they have their Reaction and meet the required conditions.

6.11 · Damage and Hit Points

When an attack hits, the character rolls the Damage Die specified by the weapon or Technique used.

For Techniques, the Damage Die normally corresponds to their Grade.

Technique GradeDamage Die
d4d4
d6d6
d8d8
d10d10
d12d12
d20d20

The result is subtracted from the target's Hit Points.

Remaining HP = current HP − damage taken

Any additional dice, multipliers and modifiers come from effects that actually apply to the attack.

Technique costs

A Technique used without enhancements does not automatically require Stamina.

Additional Builder effects instead require the costs listed in their respective descriptions.

When a Special Move contains multiple effects, all applicable costs must be paid. The Special Move grants no implicit discounts.

Calculation order

When multiple effects modify the same damage, apply them in the following order:

  1. increases or reductions in dice Grade;
  2. addition or removal of dice;
  3. rolling the dice and adding up the damage;
  4. multipliers;
  5. halving;
  6. fixed numerical reductions or increases.

Compatible effects stack.

Two distinct effects doubling the same damage therefore produce ×4, unless one of the abilities explicitly states it cannot stack or specifies a different method.

A specific rule setting its own order takes precedence over this general sequence.

6.12 · Critical conditions

Downed

When a character reaches 0 HP, they become Downed and normally leave the combat.

Reaching 0 HP does not automatically cause death.

A Downed character cannot act normally unless an ability explicitly states otherwise.

A companion can move them to safety if the situation and physical conditions permit it.

Healing, Doctor abilities and other effects restoring HP can bring them above 0 HP and restore consciousness under their respective rules.

Death can occur only when, after the character is already Downed, an enemy deliberately continues acting against them, or when a circumstance in the scene concretely threatens their life. Resolving these situations is the Game Master's responsibility.

If the character survives to the end of combat and has not been healed, they regain consciousness with 1 HP.

Downed is not equivalent to the Crashed Status.

6.12.1 · Stabilisation

A Downed character can be Stabilised to bring the wounds that reduced them to 0 HP under control.

  • Activation: Action.
  • Target: a character at 0 HP who can be physically reached.
  • Check: Cunning + Medicine.
  • Ordinary Threshold: 8.

If the Check succeeds, the character is Stabilised.

Stabilising does not restore HP or return the character to consciousness.

An ability may exceptionally keep a Stabilised character conscious at 0 HP. In this case, the character follows the specific rules of the ability used; being Stabilised alone does not normally allow them to act.

The wounds that reduced them to 0 HP no longer pose an immediate threat to their life on their own.

Stabilisation does not, however, protect against new dangers. A character can still take damage, be deliberately attacked, suffocate, drown, burn, suffer the effects of a still-active poison or face other conditions capable of killing them.

If an ability or object allows Stabilising without a Check, Stabilisation automatically succeeds.

Stabilisation does not count as Field Treatment.

The normal rule still applies: a character who survives to the end of combat without being healed regains consciousness with 1 HP.

6.12.2 · Persistent Consequences

Not every consequence of a wound is represented by losing HP or by a Status.

Fractures, embedded bullets, particularly serious injuries, diseases and other long-term conditions can be represented as Persistent Consequences.

A Persistent Consequence arises only when a rule, hazard or the Game Master explicitly assigns it.

Reaching 0 HP, suffering a Critical hit or losing many HP does not automatically generate a Persistent Consequence.

Each Persistent Consequence must specify at least:

Cause · Effect · Treatment · Healing condition

When a Check is needed, the Consequence can specify its own Threshold. If it does not, the Game Master uses the normal Thresholds from Chapter 5.

Recovering HP does not automatically remove a Persistent Consequence.

Likewise, an effect removing a negative Status does not automatically set a fracture, extract a bullet or cure a disease.

A Consequence ends when its treatment conditions are met.

Diseases

A disease can specify its own duration, symptoms, Threshold, progression and treatment.

There is no compulsory universal progression for all diseases: a simple disease may resolve through rest and treatment, while an important disease may have its own sheet.

Fatigue

A character's ordinary fatigue is mainly represented by Stamina and the Rest rules.

A form of persistent fatigue exists only when a rule or a specific Consequence introduces it.

6.13 · Statuses

Techniques, Signatures, Talents, poisons and other abilities can apply Statuses.

When an ability explicitly modifies a Status's duration, damage, Save or operation, that specific rule takes precedence over the general rule given here.

If an effect grants a Save without specifying its timing, the first Save is made when the Status is applied. If the Status remains, the target can repeat the Save at the end of each of their turns unless stated otherwise.

For Statuses granting a Save, the Attribute is listed here; the Threshold comes from the original source under §1.4 and remains fixed while that cause persists. Individual Thresholds and specific procedures take precedence.

StatusEffectDamageSave / Removal
Off-BalanceThe character cannot use Reactions.—Technique against the source's Threshold.
BleedingThe character continues losing blood until the wound is treated.1d4 HP at the start of their turn.It has no automatic Save. It requires Medicine, appropriate Healing or an ability explicitly treating the haemorrhage. A generic effect removing negative Statuses does not automatically stop bleeding that is still active.
PoisonedThe character suffers the effects of the substance that poisoned them.If the poison specifies no damage of its own, 1d6 HP at the start of the turn.Strength against the poison's Threshold. Each poison can have different damage, effects and Thresholds.
BlindedAttacks and Checks depending on sight are made with Disadvantage. They cannot normally use abilities requiring a clear line of sight.—Technique against the source's Threshold.
TerrifiedThey cannot voluntarily approach the source of fear, and their attacks are made with Disadvantage.—Spirit against the source's Threshold.
StunnedThey cannot use an Action, Bonus Action or Reaction. They can still use their Movement.—Strength against the source's Threshold.
ParalysedThey cannot use Movement, Action, Bonus Action or Reaction.—Strength against the source's Threshold.
AsleepThey cannot use Movement, Action, Bonus Action or Reaction.—Spirit against the source's Threshold. Taking damage or an effect explicitly waking the target normally ends the Status.
ImmobilisedMovement is 0. The character can continue taking other permitted actions.—Strength or Technique, depending on how they try to break free, against the source's Threshold or the restraint's specific Threshold. A Grapple resolved by a contest keeps its own procedure.
RestrainedThe character cannot voluntarily move away from the creature or effect restraining them.—Normally an opposed Strength Check against the source of the Grapple; it does not automatically use the Threshold table. A specific procedure, such as Mechanical Trap, takes precedence.
CrashedThe character has been knocked to the ground. They cannot move normally until they get up.—No general Save to get up: spend 1 Action. If an ability explicitly grants a Save to avoid Crashed, use the source's Threshold.

6.13.1 · A Status and its cause

The Status applied to a character and the cause producing it are two different elements.

An effect allowing a negative Status to be removed normally removes only a removable Status.

A Status is removable when it can cease without first removing a physical, environmental or chemical cause that continues imposing it.

If the cause remains present, generic Status removal does not automatically destroy, neutralise or modify that cause.

Examples

  • A character Blinded by a momentary flash can normally remove Blinded.
  • A character Blinded because their eyes are covered by a blindfold does not regain sight through generic Status removal: they must first uncover their eyes.
  • Permanent loss of an eye is not an ordinary Blinded Status, but a Persistent Consequence.
  • A character Immobilised by chains is not freed by an effect removing a Status: the chains must be opened, broken or removed in another way.
  • A character Blinded by a persistent cloud can momentarily remove the Status only if the effect allows it, but the cloud may apply it again under its own rules.
  • Removing Poisoned does not automatically neutralise the substance in the body. If the poison continues applying its effects, it must be neutralised with an antidote or another appropriate ability.

When an ability explicitly treats the cause too — for example, Medicine stopping bleeding or an antidote neutralising a toxin — it can also remove the associated Status.

Repeated Statuses

The same Status does not automatically multiply its effects.

Two identical applications of Bleeding, Stunned, Blinded or another Status do not produce two copies of the same penalty or damage. If they have different durations, retain the longest duration still applicable.

Different effects producing the same Status can nevertheless retain their specific properties separately.

In particular, different poisons can be active at the same time. Each poison keeps its own damage, Threshold, duration and any additional effects.

Applying the same poison multiple times does not automatically multiply a single ongoing effect, however, unless the poison's description explicitly states otherwise.

Different Statuses can coexist as normal.

Downed is not a Status. The Downed condition caused by reaching 0 HP follows the rules of § 6.12 and must not be confused with Crashed.

6.14 · Terrain and Cover

The environment can modify combatants' Movement options and defences.

Difficult Terrain

Difficult Terrain requires double Movement.

A character with 10 metres can cross only 5 of them while moving over difficult terrain.

A Charge cannot be performed through Difficult Terrain.

Cover

CoverBenefit
PartialPD +2
FullPD +4

A barrel, a ship's side or the corner of a building can offer Partial Cover.

Full Cover can also block the line of fire. The GM determines whether the attack is physically possible, considering any Techniques that allow obstacles to be bypassed.

6.15 · From naval combat to boarding

Battles between ships use distance bands.

TierDistanceOptions
Alongside0–20 mBoarding and personal combat
Under FireUp to 200 mCannon shots at full effectiveness
In SightUp to 600 mLong-range cannon shots, Threshold +4
On the HorizonBeyond 600 mPursuit and manoeuvres

A manoeuvre normally allows you to gain or lose one band.

When the ships reach the Alongside band, characters can begin boarding using the personal combat rules.

At that point, the character's Movement, weapon reach, Actions, Bonus Actions and Reactions become fundamental again.

The full naval battle rules are covered in the chapter devoted to the Ship.

Chapter 7 ·

Techniques and the Builder

On the Grand Line, two pirates can wield the same sword without fighting the same way.

One concentrates all their strength into a single slash. Another uses speed to reach the target. A third uses a Technique capable of dragging the enemy into a wall.

Techniques represent the moves a character has developed. The Technique Builder allows their characteristics to be defined through common rules, leaving the player free to choose a name, appearance and style of execution.

A Technique determines what the character can do. A Special Move, instead, combines a Technique with other abilities already possessed, without creating new ones.

The Builder and Role Manuals

The Builder presented in this chapter defines the general rules used to create and resolve Techniques. Forms, Effects, templates, costs and other options describe the system's possibilities as a whole and are not automatically available in the same way to every Role.

Each Role has its own characteristics, equipment, Talents and combat methods. The relevant Role Manuals specify which Builder options are normally compatible with that Style, which require particular conditions and which can be obtained only through Talents, equipment, powers or other specific abilities.

This manual therefore provides the general structure of the Signature Move Builder; to build a Technique for a specific Role, all the restrictions and possibilities listed in the relevant Role Manual must also be observed.

When a Role's specific rule modifies or limits a general Builder rule, the specific rule takes precedence.

7.1 · What is a Technique?

A Technique is a specific character ability usable in combat or in situations covered by its description.

Every Technique has its own identity and Grade.

For example, Oni Giri does not represent the general ability to use a sword: it is a particular attack developed by the character.

A Technique's structure

ElementFunction
NameIdentifies the move.
AttributeDetermines the die added to the Technique.
SourceIndicates where it comes from.
FormDefines how it is performed.
GradeDetermines the Technique Die and Damage Die.
EffectsGrant specific properties.
CostIndicates the Stamina required.
DescriptionDefines the move's narrative appearance.

Techniques are created and modified through the Builder, observing the character's requirements.

7.2 · The Technique's Grade

Every Technique has a Grade expressed by a die.

Progression follows the system's general scale:

The Technique's Grade is linked to the Attribute used to perform it.

A Technique can never have a Grade higher than its associated Attribute, but can voluntarily have a lower Grade.

For example, a character with Technique d10 can have a d6 Technique, a d8 Technique or a d10 Technique. They cannot have a d12 Technique based on Technique until their Attribute reaches at least d12.

No Technique, racial or Custom, grows by spending Ability Points.

When the associated Attribute increases in Grade, the character can choose to increase a linked Technique up to the new limit as well. The increase is not compulsory: a Technique can remain at a lower Grade even when the Attribute is higher.

When the Technique's Grade increases, its Technique Die used for the roll, its Damage Die, the Effect Slots available at that Grade and the Builder effects it can access normally increase.

Effects are not acquired automatically: the Technique must be updated through the Builder, observing slots, Source, Form, requirements and costs.

The die serves two functions:

Technique Die: added to the appropriate Attribute during the attack roll.

Damage Die: determines the base damage dealt when the Technique hits.

Both values grow together.

TechniqueAttack rollBase damage
d4Attribute + d41d4
d6Attribute + d61d6
d8Attribute + d81d8
d10Attribute + d101d10
d12Attribute + d121d12
d20Attribute + d201d20

The Damage Die can be modified by specific effect conditions, Talents and other applicable abilities. The ordinary Builder does not allow additional raw damage dice to be purchased.

Attribute limit

A Technique's Grade cannot exceed its Attribute's Grade.

A character with Strength d8 cannot have a Strength-based Technique of Grade d10.

To improve that Technique, they must first develop the necessary Attribute.

Devil Fruit limit

Techniques derived from a Devil Fruit must also respect the Fruit Die.

A Fruit Technique cannot exceed either its own Attribute or the Fruit Die.

If a character has Spirit d12 and Fruit Die d8, Spirit-based Fruit Techniques can reach at most d8.

7.3 · Techniques at creation

At creation, a character has their Racial Technique and can create up to two Custom Techniques.
