EngineerCunningRepairs and technical management.
Onboard fighterStrength or TechniqueBoarding and ship defence.

The station must be assigned to a crew member.

The benefits of Role Talents apply only when their respective conditions are met.

An experienced Navigator does not automatically grant their Navigation die to manoeuvres if they are not at the helm or do not possess the ability that allows it.

13.7.1 · Manoeuvre formula

Manoeuvres normally use:

Manoeuvre roll

Ship Die + Attribute

The ship statistic and Attribute depend on the station.

For example, a cannon shot uses Artillery and the Gunner’s Technique.

An Evasive Manoeuvre uses Sails and the Helmsman’s Cunning.

Abilities that add dice to the formula are applied separately.

13.7.2 · Crew and Manoeuvres

A station indicates who can make the Check required by the Manoeuvre.

Having several sailors at the same station does not automatically multiply the ship’s number of Manoeuvres.

NPC crew members can:

  • Occupy vacant stations.
  • Make the rolls specified on their sheet.
  • Execute an available ship Manoeuvre.

Any “one or two rolls per turn” granted by an NPC crew sheet indicate how many Checks that group can make within allowed activities, not how many additional naval Manoeuvres the ship gains.

A character may be able to reach several stations, but with only one main Action they can normally perform only one.

Available Manoeuvres and available Actions are required.

Cannons

The Artillery die represents the overall power of the battery used for that Cannon Shot, not the exact number of physical cannons on deck.

An ability that makes “a cannon act twice” refers to the Artillery system or Module actually used; it does not automatically multiply the Cannon Shot by every decorative cannon on the ship.

13.8 · Navigation

Navigation does not simply mean proceeding in the desired direction.

The crew must find a course, exploit the wind, recognise dangers and keep the ship operational throughout the voyage.

The Navigator is the character responsible for these activities.

13.8.1 · The voyage roll

The voyage uses the ship’s Sails die and the Helmsman’s Cunning.

Voyage roll

Sails + Cunning

Nautical instruments and compatible Talents can add dice.

The GM establishes the Threshold according to the course, weather and dangers.

There is no need to make a roll for every hour of sailing.

A Check can represent a significant part of the journey or a single particularly difficult passage.

13.8.2 · The Navigator at the helm

The Hand on the Helm Talent allows the Navigation die to be added to the voyage.

Any instruments can add further dice according to their descriptions.

13.9 · Navigation instruments

Nautical instruments can improve the voyage.

Their effect depends on quality and intended use.

InstrumentBenefit
Standard compass+d4 to Navigation rolls in the Blue Seas.
Handmade Log Pose+d6 to Navigation rolls on the Grand Line.
Updated nautical chart+d6 to the next roll on the charted course.
Detailed nautical chart+d8 to rolls on the course; reusable for three voyages.
Handmade Eternal Pose+d10 to rolls towards the set island.

Instruments must be usable in the region being crossed.

An ordinary compass does not replace a Log Pose on the Grand Line.

13.9.1 · Log Pose

A Log Pose allows an island’s magnetic field to be followed.

Once the destination is reached, it must be calibrated according to the conditions specified by the device and the course.

The handmade catalogue model requires 2d6 days of calibration.

This value belongs to the specific model and does not automatically constitute the universal time for every Log Pose in existence.

13.9.2 · Eternal Pose

An Eternal Pose permanently points to a single island.

It requires no calibration during the journey.

However, it is not an instrument that allows any destination to be freely chosen: its direction remains the one for which it was prepared.

13.10 · Dangers during the voyage

The GM determines which obstacles arise during navigation.

They may include storms, unpredictable currents, shoals, fog, sea creatures or other dangers.

A Navigation Check allows them to be tackled, but the consequences depend on the situation.

The system has no universal table imposing a random encounter on every voyage.

13.10.1 · The Check margin

The margin indicates how well the crew handled the course.

MarginOutcome
Below −3Serious failure
−3 / −1Partial failure
0Barely successful
+1 / +3Success
+4 or higherFull success

A success allows the relevant section to be crossed.

A failure can cause a complication consistent with the journey, such as a diversion, a delay, ship damage or exposure to a danger.

There is no fixed damage for a failed voyage. The GM must determine the consequence according to the obstacle faced.

Navigator Signature · Perfect Course

When you achieve a margin of at least +4 on a Navigation Check related to a crossing, you find a particularly favourable route.

You gain both of the following benefits:

Prepared course. Until the destination is reached, the crew keeps 1d6 of Course. Before making a Check directly related to the same crossing, the Navigator can consume this die and add it to the Check.

For example, this could involve:

  • Overcoming a current.
  • Facing a stretch of storm.
  • Avoiding a shoal.
  • Staying on course during an unexpected event.
  • Making another crossing Check required by the voyage.

The die must be declared before the roll.

Only one Perfect Course can be kept at a time. Gaining the Signature again before using the die replaces the previous one.

Anticipated danger. The Game Master also reveals a real danger further along the route, if there is one that the course could reasonably allow you to foresee.

If the entire crossing is resolved with a single Check and no further Checks are required before arrival, the Course Die simply goes unused. The Signature's informational benefit still applies.

13.11 · Navigator Talents at sea

Some Talents modify how the crew tackles the voyage.

TalentBenefit
Eye of the SkyPredict the weather with Advantage; if you anticipate a weather danger, the GM indicates its nature, origin and one concrete consequence.
Master CourseAnticipate a danger when planning the course, even without achieving a +4 margin.
CartographerYour charts count as detailed charts: +d8 and reusable.
Sound the DepthsExploit shoals and shallow waters to take passages inaccessible to larger ships.
Impossible CourseCross extreme routes that other ships must go around.
Harness the StormAt the helm, you and the ship ignore penalties to Navigation and Manoeuvres due to rain, natural fog, rough seas, strong winds and storms; physical effects remain.
Never LostYou always know where you are and can find places you have already visited again.

These abilities do not automatically require a new naval statistic to be introduced.

They modify Checks, consequences and possibilities for the voyage according to their descriptions.

13.12 · Naval combat

When two ships enter a conflict, naval statistics are used.

The reference unit is no longer the individual character, but the ship with its crew.

Fundamental rule

One manoeuvre per turn

Every ship normally performs one manoeuvre during its naval turn.

Talents that explicitly grant additional manoeuvres are an exception.

During combat, the crew chooses whether to attack, avoid cannon shots, ram, flee or perform an emergency repair.

Individual stations determine who makes the Check.

Additional Manoeuvres and crew Actions

An ability that grants an additional Manoeuvre to the ship increases the maximum number of naval Manoeuvres the ship can perform in that round.

However, it does not automatically grant an additional Action to characters.

Each Manoeuvre must be executed by a character or crew member who:

  • Occupies a compatible station.
  • Can actually execute the Manoeuvre.
  • Has the necessary Action, unless the ability expressly says otherwise.

If two characters occupy different stations, each can therefore spend their Action to execute one of the available Manoeuvres.

A single character cannot use two Manoeuvres in the same round merely because the ship has two, unless they also have the necessary Actions.

An ability that grants an additional roll does not instead amount to an additional Manoeuvre. The roll modifies or completes the Manoeuvre indicated by the ability.

13.13 · Naval distances

Naval battles use four bands.

TierDistanceOptions
Alongside0–20 mBoarding and personal combat.
Under FireUp to 200 mCannon shots at full effectiveness.
In SightUp to 600 mLong-range cannon shots.
On the HorizonBeyond 600 mPursuit and navigation.

A manoeuvre normally allows one distance band to be gained or lost when the situation permits.

Distance in metres remains important to determine the possibilities of a character who directly attacks another ship through a Technique.

Naval bands do not automatically increase the reach of personal weapons.

13.14 · Naval manoeuvres

The five fundamental manoeuvres are Cannon Shots, Evasive Manoeuvre, Ramming, Escape and Emergency Repair.

Reference Thresholds are normally 8, unless firing distances or other conditions differ.

13.14.1 · Cannon Shots

Cannon Shots

Offensive

Station: Gunner.

Roll: Artillery + Technique.

Damage: 1 Artillery Die, unless Modules or abilities modify it.

TierDistanceThreshold
Alongside0–20 m8
Under Fireover 20 m up to 200 m8
In Sightover 200 m up to 600 m12
On the Horizonover 600 mNormally out of reach

In Sight represents long-range fire and therefore applies the normal increase of +4 to the ordinary Threshold of 8.

Beyond 600 metres, a Cannon Shot is not normally possible unless a Module or ability expressly grants greater range.

If the roll meets or exceeds the required Threshold, the cannon shot hits.

The enemy ship loses SP equal to the Artillery die result.

Example: a ship with d8 Artillery hits an opponent.

The Gunner rolls 1d8 to determine damage. They get 6.

The enemy ship loses 6 SP.

Any Modules, such as a heavy battery, may modify damage when used.

Target

An ordinary Cannon Shot targets the ship, not an individual character on deck.

Deliberately aiming at a person requires a situation in which the target is actually identifiable and exposed, and is resolved as an attack against a character, not a normal Cannon Shot against SP.

13.14.2 · Evasive Manoeuvre

Evasive Manoeuvre

Defensive

Station: Helmsman.

Roll: Sails + Cunning.

Ordinary Threshold: 8.

Success: the Threshold of the next enemy cannon shot against the ship increases by 4.

The Evasive Manoeuvre does not make the ship permanently harder to hit.

The benefit concerns the next cannon shot against it.

Born Helmsman

The Navigator can improve the Evasive Manoeuvre through their Talents.

TalentBenefit
Born Helmsman · BasicAdds the Navigation die to evasive manoeuvres.
Born Helmsman · ImprovedOnce per naval battle, completely cancels an incoming cannon shot.
Born Helmsman · MasteryThe ship can manoeuvre and fire in the same turn without penalty.

Cancelling the cannon shot is a specific effect of the improved Talent, not an automatic consequence of every Evasive Manoeuvre.

13.14.3 · Ramming

Ramming

Offensive

Station: Captain.

Roll: Hull + Strength.

Damage dealt: the Hull die result.

Backlash: your own ship still loses 1d4 SP.

Ramming requires the ships to be in a position that physically allows the collision.

The roll determines whether the manoeuvre succeeds.

On a success, the enemy ship takes the Hull damage.

The ramming ship still loses 1d4 SP.

Steel Ram

The Steel Ram Module increases ramming damage by one step.

The benefit belongs to the Module: it does not apply to ships without it.

13.14.4 · Escape

Escape

Manoeuvre

Station: Helmsman.

Roll: Sails + Cunning.

Failure: the enemy gains a cannon shot of opportunity.

Escape allows an attempt to move away from the opponent.

The result determines whether the ship manages to gain distance or remains exposed.

If the manoeuvre fails, the enemy ship gains the specified cannon shot of opportunity.

No personal Reaction attack is introduced: the cannon shot of opportunity is an effect of the naval manoeuvre.

13.14.5 · Emergency Repair

Emergency Repair

Support · Naval battle

Station: Engineer.

Requirement: the Carpenter’s Emergency Repair or another ability that expressly allows hull repair in battle.

Check: Structure + Cunning.

Threshold: 8.

Emergency Repair is a naval manoeuvre and uses the Action of the Carpenter executing it.

Repair tools and materials must be available onboard.

If the Check succeeds, the ship recovers 1 Carpentry Die in SP.

Recovery cannot exceed Maximum SP.

If the Check result exceeds the Threshold by at least +4, the Signature A Repair That Holds may activate.

Using Emergency Repair normally counts as the ship’s manoeuvre for that turn, unless abilities grant further manoeuvres.

Ordinary repairs outside battle instead continue to use the Shipyard tables and are not replaced by this procedure.

13.14.6 · Characters, ships and two damage scales

HP and SP represent two different scales, but use the same numerical values when an attack can actually damage its target.

Personal attack against a ship. A character can deal SP only if their weapon, Technique or ability is physically capable of damaging a naval structure.

If it can, damage is rolled normally and the result is subtracted from SP at a 1:1 ratio.

A blow unable to seriously damage wood, metal or the structure concerned may deal no SP even if it would be perfectly effective against a creature.

Cannon Shot against a character. An ordinary Cannon Shot against the ship does not automatically deal HP to characters on deck.

If an individual artillery shot actually targets a character, roll Artillery + Technique against their normal Defence and, if it hits, deal 1 Artillery Die in HP.

Cover, Active Defence and abilities able to intercept the shot work normally when compatible.

Hull damage and people. Losing SP does not automatically deal the same amount of HP to the crew.

Explosions, splinters, collapses and other dangers can nevertheless produce separate consequences for characters, determined by the concrete source of the attack.

13.15 · Naval battle example

Two ships meet on the open sea.

The characters’ ship has:

The North Star

Example ship

HULL

d8

SAILS

d6

ARTILLERY

d6

STRUCTURE

d6

Structure Points: 40/40.

Module Slots: 2.

The enemy ship opens fire.

The Navigator decides to perform an Evasive Manoeuvre.

They roll Sails d6 + Cunning d8 and get a total of 11.

The ordinary Threshold is 8: the manoeuvre succeeds with a +3 margin.

The next enemy cannon shot against the North Star will have its Threshold increased by 4.

When the enemy ship fires, the difficulty is therefore higher.

On the next turn, the crew decides to use its cannon shots.

The Gunner rolls Artillery d6 + Technique d8.

If they reach the Threshold required by the distance, the shot hits and deals the Artillery die result.

If the North Star possessed Fair Winds, it could gain an additional manoeuvre per round when the conditions of the Talent are met.

13.16 · Ship Modules

The ship can be improved by installing Modules.

These devices are distinct from the body Modules in Chapter 12.

A naval Module takes up a certain number of ship slots and grants a specific property.

It can improve Artillery, increase resilience, support manoeuvres or provide equipment for the crew.

13.16.1 · Module Slots

Slots depend on the Structure statistic.

StructureAvailable slots
d41
d62
d83
d105
d127

Each Module indicates how many slots it takes up.

Not all Modules require only one slot.

An onboard Workshop, for example, takes up two.

Slot rule

You cannot install a Module if its slot cost exceeds the capacity still available.

Improving the Structure statistic can increase the ship’s slots.

13.16.2 · Module properties

Naval Modules have effects defined by their sheets.

Some examples:

ModuleEffect
Twin CannonsOne extra cannon shot per scene.
Armoured HullReduces damage taken from cannon shots by 2.
Twin Rudder+1 die to the Evasive Manoeuvre.
Navigator’s Cabin+1 die to Navigation rolls.
Onboard WorkshopRepairs: −25% hours and +1 die.
Watertight CompartmentsThe ship sinks only at −10 SP.

The complete catalogue remains in the Ship Portal.

Installing a Module does not automatically modify ship statistics unless the effect expressly specifies it.

13.16.3 · Complete naval Module sheet

Every naval Module must explicitly indicate:

Name · Slots · Activation · Target or affected system · Numerical value · Duration or frequency · Any consumption · Limitations

Formulas such as “+1 die”, “+initiative”, “+cargo” or “automatic escape” must be completed before use.

  • A dice bonus must indicate which die is added and to which Check.
  • A Cargo bonus must indicate how many Hold Units are added.
  • An Initiative bonus must indicate the value or die added.
  • An automatic Manoeuvre must specify which Manoeuvre it replaces and which conditions it must still respect.

“Automatic escape”, for example, means the normal Escape Check is not required; it does not allow you to pass through dry land, a physical barrier, a ship completely blocking the passage or another condition that makes moving away physically impossible.

13.17 · The Shipyard

The Shipyard allows the ship to be repaired and its statistics improved.

The four statistics are upgraded separately.

Improvement requires materials, a Check and an appropriate Threshold.

The ordinary Check uses:

Shipyard Check

Cunning + Carpentry

The materials required depend on the statistic being improved and the new Grade.

13.17.1 · Improving a statistic

ImprovementThreshold
d4 → d68
d6 → d810
d8 → d1012
d10 → d1214

When the blueprint succeeds, the relevant statistic increases by one step.

Example: the crew wants to improve Sails from d6 to d8.

The blueprint requires Threshold 10 and the materials specified for the new Grade.

The Engineer rolls Cunning + Carpentry.

If successful, Sails increases to d8.

The increase does not automatically modify Hull, Artillery or Structure.

13.17.2 · Permanent ship improvements

When a Carpenter Talent permanently modifies a ship through structural work, the benefit belongs to the ship, not the character.

Once the work is completed:

  • The Carpenter does not have to remain onboard for the benefit to continue working.
  • Selling the ship also transfers its improvements.
  • Changing ships does not automatically transfer improvements to the new one.
  • The permanent loss or destruction of the ship means losing its corresponding improvements.

Two characters possessing the same Talent cannot apply a benefit declared unique to the same ship several times.

Naval statistics retain the normal maximum of d12 unless an ability expressly declares it can exceed it.

Repairing or restoring the same ship normally preserves its already-installed improvements. If the vessel is completely rebuilt as a new ship, it is treated as a new hull.

13.18 · Naval repairs

Repairs can concern the hull or a specific component.

The work sheet indicates hours, Threshold, materials, success result and consequences of a serious failure.

13.18.1 · Repairing the hull

WorkHoursThreshold
Light damage, 1–10 SP87
Damaged condition168
Severe condition3210
Critical condition4812

Ordinary repair requires the specified work and materials.

Light damage allows lost SP to be recovered according to the corresponding sheet.

With a +4 margin, light damage repair also recovers 2 additional SP, without exceeding the maximum.

For work on conditions, the sheet indicates improvement in the state and, where specified, additional SP recovery.

The GM must record SP and condition consistently, since the latter follows from the percentage of SP remaining.

13.18.2 · Repairing components

ComponentHoursThresholdResult
Mainmast2410Restores the full Sails die.
Rudder169Restores normal manoeuvres.
Cannon battery2010Restores the full Artillery die.
Hold128Stops the consequences of a damaged hold.
Engine room209Restores normal movement.
Hull below the waterline3212Restores the ordinary maximum SP.

The required materials are stated on the repair sheet in the Ship Portal.

A serious failure can worsen the damage: for example, a failed cannon-battery repair may cause an explosion dealing 1d6 SP to the ship.

Consequences depend on the specific work.

13.19 · Engineer Talents in naval battle

Engineer Talents can radically change the ship’s ability to withstand damage.

TalentBenefit
Emergency RepairWith the Emergency Repair manoeuvre, the ship recovers 1 Carpentry Die in SP.
Hands on Wood · BasicEmergency Repairs recover +1 Carpentry Die in SP.
Hands on Wood · ImprovedWith the same Action, repair the hull and a damaged Module.
Hands on Wood · MasteryOnce per battle, restore a destroyed Module.
Reinforced HullThe Damage Dice of cannon shots suffered by the ship decrease by one step.
Travelling ShipyardRepair and upgrade the ship on the open sea, in half the time.
Module MasterThe ship gains one additional Module slot.

These benefits require possession of the respective Talents.

The Engineer does not automatically gain them merely by occupying the technical station.

13.20 · Boarding

When the ships reach the Alongside band, the crew can attempt to board the enemy vessel.

Boarding connects naval combat to personal combat.

Characters use the rules of Chapter 6 once the encounter on the decks begins.

13.20.1 · Preparing to board

The crew must create a concrete opportunity to cross.

They can approach, use grappling hooks or exploit an appropriate Module.

Boarding Harpoons grant +1 die to boarding and allow the enemy ship to be held.

The Carpenter’s Boarding Bridge Talent instead allows gangways and grappling hooks to be prepared through an Action.

The crew can cross them without making rolls and without provoking Reaction attacks during the crossing specified by the Talent.

13.20.2 · Combat on the decks

Once onboard, every character uses the normal personal rules.

Movement depends on Race, not the Sails die.

A pirate can use Techniques, Talents, Haki and Special Moves, respecting their Action economy.

The naval manoeuvre does not automatically grant an additional Action to every fighter.

The GM manages the sequence between ship actions and character actions according to the situation.

13.21 · Naval combat order

When two ships face each other, combat is organised into naval rounds.

Every ship has its own turn and can normally perform one manoeuvre per round. Talents and Modules that expressly grant additional manoeuvres are an exception.

13.21.1 · Naval initiative

At the start of the encounter, each ship’s Helmsman makes a roll:

Ship initiative

Sails + Cunning

The ship with the highest result acts first.

This formula uses the characteristics already associated with initiative and naval manoeuvres.

On a tie, the GM establishes which ship acts first considering position and the initial situation. No additional numerical modifier is specified.

The order remains unchanged during the encounter unless specific effects apply.

13.21.2 · The ship’s turn

During its turn, the crew chooses a manoeuvre and assigns its execution to the appropriate station.

The procedure is as follows:

  • Declare the manoeuvre. The crew chooses Cannon Shots, Evasive Manoeuvre, Ramming, Escape or Emergency Repair.
  • Identify the station. The assigned character uses the required Attribute.
  • Make the roll. Add the naval statistic die and the Attribute die, applying any bonuses.
  • Resolve the effect. Apply damage, displacements, repairs or other specified results.
  • Pass to the next ship. The crew waits until the next round to choose another manoeuvre, unless exceptions apply.

Having several characters at stations does not grant an additional manoeuvre to each.

A Gunner and a Helmsman can both be ready, but the ship normally chooses only one manoeuvre.

Exception: Born Helmsman · Mastery allows manoeuvring and firing in the same turn without penalty. Other abilities may grant further exceptions according to their descriptions.

13.21.3 · Mixed combat

When ships fight at range and no character is directly involved in a personal encounter, the ship’s turn is normally used.

When the following begin simultaneously:

  • Boarding.
  • Fighting on deck.
  • Displacements between ships.
  • Personal attacks against crews or structures.

the scene switches to a shared personal Initiative.

All characters normally roll Technique + Cunning.

Each personal combat round also counts as a naval round.

Within that round, each ship normally retains 1 naval Manoeuvre, plus any additional Manoeuvres granted by Talents or Modules.

A Manoeuvre is executed during the personal turn of the character occupying the appropriate station and uses their main Action.

The character does not receive an additional Action because they are operating a station.

Effects lasting “one turn” or “one round”, including Haki, Statuses and upkeep, use the normal personal round.

An ability allowing a jump, movement or intervention between two ships is therefore resolved during the character’s normal turn or through the Reaction indicated by the ability.

For ships controlled mainly by NPCs, the Game Master can use a single crew turn to resolve the naval Manoeuvre without assigning an individual Initiative to every sailor.

13.22 · Approach and pursuit

A naval battle is not necessarily a static exchange of cannon shots.

One ship may try to catch another to board it. Another may try to escape combat, exploiting the wind and its manoeuvrability.

Distances are represented through the four naval bands.

Distance bands

  • Alongside · 0–20 m
    The ships are close enough to attempt boarding.
  • Under Fire · Up to 200 m
    Cannon shots can be made at ordinary distance.
  • In Sight · Up to 600 m
    Cannon shots require greater precision.
  • On the Horizon · Over 600 m
    The ships are far apart. The scene is primarily a pursuit.

13.22.1 · Changing band

A successful movement manoeuvre normally allows the distance to change by one band.

An approaching ship can move from In Sight to Under Fire.

An escaping ship can move from Under Fire to In Sight.

The manoeuvre does not automatically allow several bands to be crossed in the same turn.

A Talent or Module can modify this limit when expressly specified.

13.22.2 · Pursuit between two ships

When one ship tries to escape and the other wants to prevent it, both make an opposed Check:

Sails + Cunning

The escaping ship uses its Helmsman. The pursuing ship uses its Helmsman to maintain contact.

The highest result determines who manages to impose their manoeuvre.

OutcomeConsequence
The escaping ship winsDistance increases by one band.
The pursuing ship winsEscape is prevented; distance does not increase.
TieThe ships remain in the current band.

This procedure makes the resolution of opposed manoeuvres explicit, which previously had no dedicated formula.

Cannon shot of opportunity

If Escape fails, the pursuing ship gains the cannon shot of opportunity specified by the manoeuvre.

The cannon shot can be made only if the enemy ship is within a distance reachable by Artillery.

The cannon shot of opportunity does not automatically allow a ship already out of reach to be hit.

13.22.3 · Ending a pursuit

Reaching the On the Horizon band does not necessarily end pursuit: the two ships could still see each other.

The GM determines when contact is permanently lost, considering visibility, sea conditions, navigation instruments and actual possibilities of continuing.

No fixed number of rounds is required to disappear over the horizon.

An immobilised ship cannot gain distance through a normal Escape manoeuvre. If the Engine Room is destroyed or other damage prevents active movement, the crew must first solve that problem or exploit an ability that expressly allows movement.

13.23 · Sinking and rescue

When the ship reaches 0 Structure Points, it enters the Sunk state.

It can no longer continue normal navigation or combat.

This state does not mean that all characters onboard die instantly.

The GM describes the consequences according to the nature of the damage: a ship could split during Ramming, take on water through a breach or progressively lose the ability to float.

13.23.1 · When the ship reaches the sinking threshold

When a ship reaches its sinking threshold, the Game Master immediately declares what type of failure is occurring.

Slow failure · 3 rounds. The ship takes on water or gradually loses buoyancy. The crew has three complete rounds to evacuate or attempt intervention. Rapid failure · 1 round. A serious breach or structural failure leaves only one complete round before the ship is lost. Catastrophic failure · immediate. The ship is split, explodes or instantly loses the ability to stay afloat. Characters can use only Reactions or abilities that declare they can intervene at that moment.

The GM chooses the profile according to the cause of the damage and must communicate it clearly when the ship reaches the threshold.

A repair that brings the ship above its sinking threshold before failure can stop the process if the physical cause allows it.

13.23.2 · Abandoning ship

If sinking leaves time to react, characters can attempt to save themselves.

The possibilities depend on the tools available and the situation.

A lifeboat can allow the vessel to be left. A character could reach a nearby ship, cling to a wreck or use a mobility Technique.

When the outcome is uncertain, the GM requires an appropriate Check, such as Athletics, Acrobatics or another relevant Skill.

Distances and Movement follow the ordinary rules.

Devil Fruit users remain subject to their weakness to the sea. They cannot ignore it merely because they are trying to abandon a ship.

Evacuation

During each available round, a character can normally use Movement and Action to:

  • Reach a lifeboat.
  • Free a companion.
  • Retrieve an object.
  • Jump onto another ship.
  • Attempt a repair.
  • Use a mobility ability.

Passengers not managed individually are evacuated through crew actions and available resources; if the outcome is uncertain, the GM requires an appropriate Check.

13.23.3 · Watertight Compartments

Module · Watertight Compartments

The ship withstands going below the ordinary threshold of 0 SP.

It sinks only when it reaches −10 SP.

The Module modifies only the sinking threshold.

It does not restore SP or automatically repair damaged parts.

A ship at −4 SP with Watertight Compartments is not intact: it is severely compromised, but the Module allows it to stay afloat until the specified threshold.

The sinking threshold becomes −10 SP. From 0 to −9 SP, the ship is still afloat but remains in Critical condition and severely compromised.

13.23.4 · Living Figurehead

The Living Figurehead Module allows the ship to withstand a fatal blow.

Once per adventure, when a blow would bring it to the sinking threshold, it remains at 1 SP.

The benefit applies to the single fatal blow.

It does not automatically protect the ship from all subsequent attacks.

Living Figurehead prevents one fatal blow and leaves the ship at 1 SP. It does not initiate sinking. If a subsequent blow brings it to the threshold again, the normal rules apply.

13.23.5 · Carpenter Ultimate: She Will Not Sink

She Will Not Sink

Ultimate

Frequency: once per naval battle.

When the ship would fall to 0 SP or less:

  • It stays afloat with 1 SP and, until the end of the battle, cannot fall below 1 SP.
  • It can continue to perform manoeuvres made possible by the situation.
  • All Destroyed Modules temporarily become operational again.

If active, it takes precedence for its duration: the ship remains at 1 SP and does not enter the sinking process until the end of the battle.

At the end of the battle

The ship remains at 1 SP, in Critical condition; Modules return to their actual previous condition and a real Shipyard is required for structural repairs.

The Ultimate is not a permanent repair. Its complete description is in the Carpenter Talent (§ 4.9.1).

13.24 · Voyages and resources

A journey on the Grand Line may require a few hours or entire weeks.

Its duration depends on the course, sea conditions, ship and Navigator’s abilities.

The system does not assign a universal speed in kilometres per day for every Sails Grade.

13.24.1 · Establishing journey duration

Before departure, the Game Master gives the crew a voyage estimate when the characters have the necessary information.

The estimate considers distance, course, weather conditions, ship characteristics and available instruments.

The Navigator can use the Navigation Skill to assess conditions along the route.

A Log Pose allows the destination’s direction to be identified, but does not automatically remove storms, currents or other obstacles.

Dividing the journey into legs

A long voyage can be divided into several significant legs.

For example:

Example · Journey to an island

  • Departure from port
    The crew prepares the ship and puts to sea.
  • Crossing the current
    The Navigator tackles a dangerous section with a Check.
  • Open-sea navigation
    The voyage proceeds normally until a new obstacle emerges.
  • Approaching the island
    The crew must recognise the landing place and overcome any coastal dangers.

There is no need to roll dice for every calm day.

Checks are required when the result can significantly change the journey.

13.24.2 · Provisions and cargo

Provisions are recorded in the Ship’s Logbook, together with cargo and other ship resources.

They include what the crew has actually taken onboard for the voyage.

To avoid excessive bookkeeping, the GM can record supplies relative to the expected journey duration and check whether they remain sufficient when the voyage experiences a significant delay.

The initial amount of provisions must be agreed before departure, not determined retroactively solely to make things difficult for the players.

Hold Units

The Hold uses abstract Units to represent cargo, barrels, crates, materials and bulky provisions.

Ordinary capacity depends on Structure:

StructureHold Units
d48
d612
d816
d1020
d1224

Small personal items, weapons carried by characters and normal individual equipment do not take up Hold Units.

One Unit approximately represents a crate, a barrel or an equivalent amount of cargo.

Provisions

One Provision Unit supports up to 5 people for one week.

Crew and passengers are added together to determine consumption.

Example: a ship with 10 people consumes 2 Provision Units per week.

Fractions are rounded up at the end of each week of travel.

An ability that reduces provision consumption modifies this value before rounding.

Cargo and Modules

Hold Units and Module slots are completely separate resources.

Module slots represent the ship’s structural capacity to house installations.

Hold Units represent the space available to transport resources and goods.

Installing a Module does not automatically occupy Hold Units unless its sheet declares it.

Expanded Hold

The Expanded Hold Module allows more cargo and provisions to be carried.

It does not automatically produce new supplies.

The benefit increases the ship’s carrying capacity; resources must still be purchased or obtained.

Expanded Hold must expressly indicate how many Units it adds. A generic description such as “increases cargo” is not sufficient for a usable sheet.

13.24.3 · Delays and unforeseen events

Failure during a Navigation Check can change the voyage.

The GM chooses a consequence consistent with the obstacle faced: a delay, a diversion, ship damage or exposure to a new danger.

Failure does not necessarily have to cause all these consequences simultaneously.

If the ship takes damage, it is managed through Structure Points and hull conditions.

If the journey becomes longer, the GM updates the estimated duration and checks provision availability.

13.24.4 · Running out of supplies

When provisions run out, the crew must find a solution through available resources and abilities.

They can try fishing, searching for food on an island, reaching a port or using appropriate Talents.

Survival, Nature and Exploration Checks can be used when relevant.

Running out of provisions does not automatically deal a fixed amount of damage: the rules do not yet define a universal numerical progression for hunger and dehydration.

The GM describes the consequences and uses the survival rules agreed for the campaign, taking the crew’s concrete possibilities into account.

Chapter 14 ·

Galley and Cooking

A crew may possess a formidable ship and legendary weapons, but after days of sailing, battles and sleepless nights, even the hardiest pirate needs a hot meal.

In Grand Line Chronicles, Cooking allows ingredients to be turned into dishes capable of restoring Hit Points (HP) and Stamina (ST). Results depend on the recipe and the skill of whoever prepares it.

The Cook is the specialist in this system: they can tackle more ambitious recipes, improve meal benefits and prepare banquets for the whole crew.

The complete recipe book remains in the digital Galley. This chapter contains only the rules needed to use it and create new dishes.

14.1 · The Cooking Check

Preparing a dish requires a Check using:

Preparation Check

Spirit + Cooking

The result is compared with the recipe’s Threshold.

Every dish states its own Threshold. Simple recipes can be prepared with ordinary ingredients, while more ambitious ones require greater experience and unusual raw ingredients.

ThresholdPreparation type
6Simple recipe
8Ordinary recipe
10Ambitious recipe
12Particularly ambitious recipe

These values represent the Thresholds in the recipe book, not a rule forcing every new recipe to use only these four numbers.

The Game Master can approve recipes with different requirements.

The role of the Cook

The Heart and the Engine Trait reserves ambitious dishes, generally those with Threshold 10–12, for the Cook.

A character with the Cooking Skill but not the Cook Role can attempt preparations within their expertise.

Having a high Cooking die does not automatically remove this distinction between Skill and Role Trait.

14.2 · Preparing a dish

Before making the Check, the player chooses a recipe and checks that they have the necessary ingredients and conditions.

Choose the recipe. Select an official dish or use a custom recipe approved by the GM.

Check ingredients. The Galley must have the required raw ingredients.

Devote the necessary time. Preparation must occur under conditions compatible with the recipe and any Talents possessed.

Make the Check. Roll Spirit + Cooking against the dish’s Threshold.

Record the result. The prepared dish retains the outcome achieved, which determines its benefits when consumed.

A dish is prepared before being served. During combat, you cannot declare that you have cooked a recipe that was not prepared.

The Battlefield Service Talent allows an already-prepared dish to be served during the encounter, not cooked instantly.

Portions

Ingredient quantities stated by a recipe normally represent the ingredients required for one portion.

Preparing several portions proportionally multiplies the ingredients required.

The Check can be made once for a batch of the same dish prepared simultaneously; every portion of that batch retains the same outcome.

Cook’s Hand · Improved is an exception: a single portion prepared by the Cook can benefit two companions.

War Banquet still requires enough ingredients to actually feed the crew, but uses a single preparation Check for the entire banquet.

14.3 · The four Cooking outcomes

The Check value is compared with the Threshold to determine the margin.

Margin = result − Threshold

Every recipe states four possible results.

Negative margin

Failure

The dish produces the benefit indicated in the recipe’s Failure entry. It may recover nothing, but some recipes retain a minimal effect.

Margin 0

Barely successful

Preparation barely succeeds and produces the reduced benefit stated in the recipe.

Margin +1 / +3

Success

The dish produces its ordinary benefit.

Margin +4 or higher

Critical

The dish achieves the maximum result specified by the recipe. For the Cook, the Signature The Pinnacle also activates.

A Critical depends on a margin of at least +4, not simply rolling the maximum result on a single die.

Failure is not always identical

The dish sheet establishes the effects of every outcome.

Some recipes state no recovery on failure. Others may retain a small benefit.

The GM must not replace the recipe’s Failure entry with an invented penalty.

14.4 · Recovering HP and Stamina

Dishes can recover HP, ST or both resources.

The amount depends on the outcome achieved during preparation.

ResourceHow it works
HPThe character recovers the indicated Hit Points.
STThe character recovers the indicated Stamina.
HP and STBoth recoveries stated in the recipe apply.

Recovery cannot exceed the ordinary maximum value of the resource unless an ability expressly specifies increasing its maximum.

A dish restoring 4 ST does not permanently increase the character’s maximum Stamina.

A Talent such as The Legendary Banquet, however, can explicitly modify that limit.

One recipe, different results

A dish could state:

OutcomeHPST
Critical2d6+2+3
Success1d8+2+2
Barely successful1d4+1
FailureNoneNone

This is a structural example taken from the recipe book, not a universal table applicable to every dish.

A fruit-based dish, for example, could mainly recover Stamina, while other recipes might favour HP.

Meal benefits

A character can gain HP and ST recovery from only one ordinary meal per scene.

Consuming further dishes in the same scene is possible narratively, but does not grant HP or ST recovery again.

The limit resets when a new significant scene begins or after a Rest.

Abilities that expressly declare they can serve a new meal during the encounter — such as Legendary Second Fire — are an exception and produce their effects according to their descriptions.

Two dishes granting the same temporary bonus do not stack that benefit: use the higher value.

Different bonuses can coexist when their rules allow it.

14.5 · Dish categories

The Galley organises recipes into categories.

Each category describes a family of preparations and makes appropriate ingredients easier to identify.

CategoryCharacteristics
MeatSubstantial meat-based preparations.
FishDishes based on fish and seafood.
VegetablesVegetable preparations, soups and side dishes.
FruitFresh fruit, fruit salads and drinks.
SweetsDesserts and sweet preparations.
BrothBroths, light soups and liquid preparations.

Exotic ingredients also form a particular sourcing category.

The category alone does not determine how many HP or how much ST a dish recovers. The recipe’s values always establish this.

Category bonuses

Certain ingredients and categories can provide supplementary benefits.

The Galley reference includes, among others, these cases:

ConditionStated benefit
Fresh fish+1 bonus die.
Fresh tropical fruit+1 ST to the outcome.
Exotic ingredients+1 bonus die and increased bonuses.

For benefits that do not specify which roll or recovery the die is added to, the GM must define its application before preparation.

Do not assume “+1 bonus die” automatically means +1 Hit Die, +1 Damage Die or +1 die to any Check.

The recipe and ingredient description remain the main reference.

Dice granted by ingredients

A die granted by an ingredient is never added to the Cooking Check unless the sheet expressly says it modifies the Check.

If it modifies a recovery, it must indicate which resource it modifies and which die is added.

14.6 · Special ingredients

Some ingredients possess properties that modify the dish’s result.

The bonus applies according to the conditions stated by the ingredient.

Examples of notable ingredients

Bananawani Meat

Rare

+1d6 extra HP on any outcome.

Sea King Meat

Uncommon

+1d4 HP and +1 ST on any outcome.

Deep-Sea Squid

Rare

+1d4 extra ST only on a Critical or Success.

Glowing Sea Mushroom

Rare

+2 ST on any outcome.

The description “on any outcome” also includes Failure when the ingredient can actually be used in the recipe.

An ingredient working only on Success or Critical does not instead grant its benefit on a barely successful outcome.

Ingredient bonuses do not cancel the requirements for obtaining them.

A rare sea creature could require a fishing expedition, while a legendary ingredient might be available only on a particular island.

Extra ingredients

A preparation can normally use only one Extra Ingredient beyond the ingredients required by the recipe.

Mandatory recipe ingredients do not count towards this limit.

An Extra Ingredient is consumed during preparation and applies only the benefit stated on its sheet.

Two copies of the same Extra Ingredient cannot be used to stack the same benefit several times unless a recipe expressly declares otherwise.

Different ingredients can produce different effects only when they are already part of the recipe or an ability expressly permits more than one to be used.

Availability and storage

The Galley reference distinguishes common, uncommon, rare and legendary ingredients.

Some products can be purchased in ports. Others must be caught, gathered or obtained during adventure.

The GM checks that the crew actually possesses the required ingredients.

Cooking does not create raw ingredients from nothing.

Storage

Unless the recipe states otherwise:

  • A freshly prepared dish retains its benefits for 24 hours.
  • A fresh ingredient normally keeps for 3 days.
  • Dried, salted, canned or specially preserved ingredients follow the duration stated on their sheets and can normally last for weeks.

A refrigerated Galley, suitable container or another preservation system can extend these times according to its description.

When food exceeds its storage time, it does not automatically retain the mechanical effects recorded during preparation.

14.7 · The Cook’s Signature: The Pinnacle

The Pinnacle

Signature · Margin +4

When the Cook achieves a Critical on a Cooking Check, the dish becomes a masterpiece.

Apply the recipe’s Critical result and also activate the specified Category Bonus.

The Signature does not automatically double all recipe values.

Recovery is as specified for the Critical, modified only by the benefits actually applicable.

The Battle Cook Talent can bring Category Bonus activation forward to a normal Success.

14.8 · Creating a custom dish

To create a new recipe, first choose an official reference recipe with a similar Threshold and function.

The new recipe uses that sheet as its power limit.

Define:

FieldDescription
NameDish name.
CategoryMeat, Fish, Vegetables, Fruit, Sweets or Broth.
IngredientsNecessary raw ingredients.
ThresholdPreparation difficulty.
TimeTime required.
CriticalMaximum recovery.
SuccessOrdinary recovery.
Barely successfulReduced recovery.
FailureAny minimal recovery or absence of benefits.
BonusAdditional ingredient and category properties.

A custom recipe can redistribute its recovery between HP and ST, but should not exceed the overall value of an official recipe of the same tier without requiring rarer ingredients or a higher Threshold.

An ingredient’s rarity alone does not grant a universal numerical value.

A rare ingredient produces only the bonus stated on its sheet.

Benefits belonging to Cook Talents — for example:

  • More recipients.
  • Automatic Status removal.
  • Multiplied recovery.
  • Quick service in battle.

cannot be included in a custom recipe for free.

Any original effect must be defined and approved before the Cooking Check.

The Check result decides which of the four recorded outcomes applies: it does not allow new effects to be chosen after the roll.

14.9 · Cooking during a voyage

A ship with a working Galley allows meals to be prepared during navigation when conditions permit.

The crew must have enough ingredients and the time required by the recipes.

There is no universal duration applicable to every dish.

Some preparations require little time. Elaborate recipes and banquets may require hours.

Express Cooking

The Express Cooking Talent allows the Cook to prepare a dish during a short break that would normally require hours.

The ability reduces preparation time according to its effect.

It does not make ingredients free or turn cooking into a combat Action.

The Counter

The Counter Talent allows the Cook to set up a field kitchen anywhere they have a fire and a work surface.

They suffer no normal penalties due to a lack of equipment.

The Talent does not remove the need for ingredients.

14.10 · Serving food during combat

Normally, preparation takes place before the encounter.

The Battlefield Service Talent is an exception allowing the Cook to quickly distribute an already-prepared ration.

Consuming a meal

A normal meal is not a combat item.

Outside an encounter, consuming a portion normally requires a few minutes and the benefit applies at the end of the meal.

During combat, a character cannot freely consume a normal dish with a simple Action unless an ability expressly permits it.

Battlefield Service, Sovereign Service and similar abilities are exceptions.

Battlefield Service

d8 Talent

Action · Conscious target within 5 metres.

Requirement: possess an already-prepared portion.

Serve an already prepared portion to a conscious companion within 5 metres.

Delivery and consumption are part of the Cook's same Action.

The recipient does not spend their own Action.

This ability does not include any Displacement by the Cook: the target must already be within 5 metres.

There must also be a physically possible way to deliver the food; a closed wall or another impassable obstacle prevents service.

The recipient must be conscious and physically able to eat or drink.

An Unconscious character cannot receive the benefits of a normal meal through this ability.

Serving the dish does not require a second Cooking Check: the result was already determined when the recipe was prepared.

Using the Talent does not create an additional portion or allow a different outcome to be chosen.

Second Fire

The Cook’s Second Fire Talent, available in the d12 tier, allows a ration to be thrown to a companion within 5 metres when they fall below half their HP.

The ability uses a Reaction, can be used once per encounter and immediately restores 1d6 ST to the companion.

It does not require a Bonus Action.

14.11 · Talents that improve dishes

Cook Talents modify preparation, number of recipients and meal benefits.

TalentEffect
Cook’s Pantry · d8Add the Cooking die to rolls to search or fish for ingredients.
Never on an Empty Stomach · d8Your dishes perform at least as well as a barely successful outcome, even if the Check fails.
Cook’s Hand · Basic · d8Add a die to the resource recovered by the dish, HP or ST, according to the effect. If the dish recovers both, choose only one resource at preparation.
Battle Cook · d10The Category Bonus already activates on a Success.
Speciality · d10Choose a dish from the catalogue and reduce its Threshold by 2.
Cook’s Hand · Improved · d10One preparation can be consumed by two companions; both receive the outcome.
Cook’s Hand · Mastery · d12Your dishes also remove one removable negative Status from whoever consumes them.
War Banquet · d12Prepare a meal before the encounter and apply the outcome to all companions taking part in the banquet.

Talents with prerequisites retain their progression.

Cook’s Hand · Improved requires the Basic version. Cook’s Hand · Mastery requires the Improved version.

Portions

Under ordinary conditions, a preparation benefits one recipient according to its recipe.

Cook’s Hand · Improved allows the result to be extended to two companions.

War Banquet instead allows the same outcome to be distributed to all crew members taking part in the prepared meal.

These Talents explicitly modify the number of recipients: they are not benefits automatically granted to every preparation.

14.12 · The Legendary Banquet

The Legendary Banquet

Ultimate · d20

The Cook prepares a banquet capable of restoring the strength the crew needs to face an entire adventure.

Frequency: once per adventure.

Requirements: the Cook's Ambition and the Game Master's permission.

Preparation requires hours of work, real ingredients and a crew that sits down to eat.

Immediate effects

No Cooking Check is required.

The banquet automatically counts as a Critical for every companion participating.

HP and Stamina recovery effects are doubled.

Effects until the end of the adventure

Each character who ate receives:

+2 maximum ST

The bonus temporarily modifies maximum Stamina.

The will to rise again

The first blow that would bring the character to 0 HP instead leaves them at 1 HP.

When the adventure ends, the maximum Stamina bonus ends.

Protection from the fatal blow can intervene only once for each character who took part in the banquet.

The Legendary Banquet cannot be declared retroactively after the crew has been defeated.

It requires real preparation and a dedicated narrative moment before its benefits can apply.

14.13 · Preparation example

The crew’s Cook decides to prepare a Battle Stew before setting sail.

The official recipe requires game meat, potatoes, onions and broth. It has Threshold 10 and uses Spirit + Cooking.

Battle Stew

Threshold 10

Spirit + Cooking

Meat

The Cook has d8 Spirit and d10 Cooking.

They make the roll and get 13.

The dish achieves a normal Success.

Stew benefit

1d8+2

HP recovered

+2

ST recovered

If the Cook possesses Speciality

If they chose the Stew as their Speciality, the Threshold falls from 10 to 8.

With the same result of 13, the margin becomes +5.

Preparation therefore achieves a Critical and uses the corresponding recipe benefits.

The Cook can record the preparation in the Session Log through the Galley, noting the dish made and the result achieved.

Chapter 15 ·

Nakama Bonds

15.1 · The Nakama Bond

A Nakama Bond represents the trust and understanding developed between two companions.

Every Bond begins at 0.

At 0, the Bond exists narratively but does not yet grant a die.

When it grows, it follows the progression:

0 → d4 → d6 → d8 → d10 → d12 → d20.

The Bond does not grow through Ability Points. The Game Master establishes when shared events justify an increase.

15.2 · Nakama Aid

A character can use their Bond to help a companion when their contribution is concretely possible.

A single roll can receive the Nakama die of only one companion.

During an encounter

Each character can provide Nakama Aid once per encounter.

Outside combat

Each character can use Nakama Aid once per scene.

The helped companion adds to their roll the Bond die the helper has towards them.

During combat, helping uses the helper’s Action. If help is provided outside their turn, the character gives up their next Action.

Outside combat, this Action economy does not apply unless the scene expressly uses a turn structure.

The Game Master always checks that intervention is actually possible in the situation.

Chapter 16 ·

Advancement and Upgrades

No pirate is born a legend.

You become stronger by surviving encounters, learning from your mistakes and crossing seas that seemed impossible. But growing does not just mean dealing more damage: a Navigator can learn to read unfamiliar currents, a Doctor can perfect their healing and a Captain can find the courage to lead the crew against a much stronger enemy.

In Grand Line Chronicles, character growth follows different paths:

Ability Points (AP) allow Attributes, Skills and abilities that expressly declare they use them to be developed.

Custom Techniques follow the limit of the Attribute used.

Upgrades represent major moments of growth in the story and grant resources, AP and Talents.

Fruit and Haki follow their respective narrative progressions.

These systems work together, but are not the same thing. A pirate can perfect their abilities through experience without waiting for an Upgrade. Conversely, an Upgrade grants benefits not obtained simply by accumulating AP.

16.1 · Progression dice

Attributes, Skills and Techniques use the same ordinary advancement scale. The Prestige Manual extends Attributes and Skills beyond d20: d20+d4, d20+d6, d20+d8, d20+d10, d20+d12 and d20+d20 (Saikyō). Each progression remains independent. The ordinary Technique catalogue retains its Grades, slots, costs and requirements.

Progression scale

d4 → d6 → d8 → d10 → d12 → d20

An advancement increases the die by one step.

You cannot jump directly from d4 to d10 by paying only the cost of the final step.

When a Skill die increases, that improvement applies to all Checks using the Skill.

Techniques have no AP of their own. For Custom Techniques: their Grade can be increased when the Grade of the associated Attribute allows it.

When a Technique’s Grade increases, its Technique Die, Damage Die and available Slots grow.

Increasing the die does not automatically add new effects to the Technique: the character must modify it through the Builder, respecting its requirements.

16.2 · Ability Points

AP represent mastery gained through experience and training.

They are distributed among Attributes, Skills and any abilities that expressly declare they use AP.

Techniques, both racial and Custom, neither accumulate nor spend AP of their own.

Every Attribute and Skill has an individual progression.

A pirate can therefore have many AP accumulated in Athletics and few in Navigation.

AP are not Berry and cannot be used to buy weapons, materials or Modules.

How are they gained?

There are two sources.

Experience during the story. Using a Skill in significant situations can cause it to accumulate AP. The Game Master determines how many to award.

Upgrade reward. When granting an Upgrade, the Game Master also awards an amount of AP the player can freely distribute between Attributes and Skills. Racial and Custom Techniques receive no AP.

There is no fixed amount of AP for each successful roll. Their award must reflect the pace of the campaign, without forcing players to continually repeat irrelevant Checks to accumulate points.

16.3 · Advancement thresholds

To increase a die, the character must accumulate the AP required for the next step.

StepAP required
d4 → d610
d6 → d825
d8 → d1060
d10 → d12150
d12 → d20400

These are the manual’s reference values. The Game Master can modify them according to the expected campaign duration.

AP thresholds apply to Attributes, Skills and abilities that expressly declare they use Ability Points.

They do not apply to Custom Techniques.

Spending AP

When a character has enough AP on an ability, they can spend them to increase its die by one step.

The necessary points are subtracted from that ability’s progression.

Any remaining AP are kept.

Example · Improving a Skill

A pirate has Athletics d6 and has accumulated 32 AP on that Skill.

Reaching d8 requires 25.

BEFORE

Athletics d6

32 P.A.

AFTER

Athletics d8

7 AP remaining

The character has reached the new Grade.

To later advance from d8 to d10, they must reach the new Threshold of 60 AP.

AP already spent are not recovered if the character decides to train a different ability.

16.4 · Improving Attributes

The four Attributes are:

AttributeScope
StrengthPhysical power and resilience.
TechniquePrecision, coordination and execution.
SpiritWill and determination.
CunningReasoning, strategy and mental readiness.

Attributes increase by spending AP according to the normal thresholds.

They do not automatically increase when the character receives an Upgrade.

The player must choose to invest part of their AP in them.

This choice involves a sacrifice: points spent on Strength cannot simultaneously be used to increase a Skill or a Technique.

Attributes and Techniques

The Attribute Grade determines the maximum Grade achievable by Techniques using it.

When an Attribute increases, linked Techniques do not automatically increase: the player can decide which to raise to the new Grade and which to keep at a lower Grade.

This increase requires no additional AP for the Technique.

For example, a character has d6 Technique and a personal d6 Technique. If the Technique Attribute rises to d8, the player can raise that personal Technique to d8: the Technique gains the Technique Die, Damage Die and Slots specified by the new Grade.

16.5 · Improving Techniques

Racial and Custom Techniques do not grow through Ability Points of their own.

Its limit is determined by the Attribute used.

When that limit increases, the player can voluntarily increase the Technique’s Grade up to the new available maximum.

Improving a Technique:

  • increases the Technique Die;
  • increases the Damage Die;
  • increases available Effect Slots;
  • makes the new Grade’s effects accessible.

A Technique is not required to reach the permitted maximum.

Increasing Grade does not automatically add new effects: the Technique must be modified through the Builder.

16.6 · Upgrades

An Upgrade represents a decisive moment in the character’s growth.

Unique Traits and Upgrades

When an Attribute and Skill meet the requirements of a Synergy, the corresponding Unique Trait becomes available. Unlocking is not acquisition. An available Unique Trait can be acquired using the normal Talent choice granted by an Upgrade. If several Traits unlock simultaneously, all become available, but each must be acquired separately (§16.9).

It can be granted at the end of a narrative arc, after an important feat or following an event that profoundly changes the pirate.

It is not automatically awarded after a predetermined number of combats.

The Game Master establishes when the story justifies an Upgrade.

16.7 · Campaign pace

The total number of Upgrades is not fixed.

The Game Master establishes it considering the campaign duration and the main moments of the crew’s growth.

In a long campaign, Upgrades can be distributed across many narrative arcs and accompanied by smaller amounts of AP.

In a short campaign, the GM can grant more AP with every Upgrade, allowing characters to reach high Grades in fewer sessions.

AP thresholds can be adapted at the start of the campaign and are editable in pirate management.

It is important for players to know the expected pace: investing 400 AP to reach d20 is a different choice in a campaign intended to last years than in a story lasting a few sessions.

16.8 · Recording growth

Pirate management allows AP, dice, Upgrades and acquired Talents to be tracked.

Synergies in growth

When the Grade of an Attribute or Skill increases, check whether the new value meets the requirements of one or more Synergies. Traits meeting the requirements are recorded as available. A Trait is recorded as acquired only after a valid choice is spent. If a Trait is an Evolution, the new benefit replaces the previous one and does not stack with it (§16.9).

When a character advances, update the relevant values and check whether the new die makes further options available in the Talent Tree or Technique Builder.

Opening new options does not mean automatically acquiring them. Prestige Talent choices open at Prestige I, III and V of the Role Skill; at Saikyō, possessed Talents improve without a fourth choice. Spirit has three separate choices. Enhancements of other Attributes follow their respective compatibility and do not consume Role choices.

A Technique can reach d12 without d12 effects having been selected yet. A Role Skill can reach d20 without the character having obtained the Ultimate.

The sheet must represent what the pirate has actually learned, not everything they could theoretically learn. When an evolved version of a Role Talent is acquired, even in ordinary progression, the previous version is no longer separately usable. It remains acquired as a prerequisite and retains only benefits incorporated in the evolution; bonuses, costs and limits do not stack. For Haki effects, however, the basic alternatives expressly specified by the Prestige Manual remain.

“It does not matter how far away the next island is. As long as there is a dream to chase and a crew to share the sea with, the journey continues.”

16.9 · Unique Traits and Synergies

Unique Traits reward the development of the sixteen Skills through Synergies between Attribute and Skill. They are distinct from Role Talents and unlock automatically when the stated requirements are met.

16.9.1 · Synergies

A Synergy is a specific combination of an Attribute and a Skill. Each Unique Trait directly states the pair and Grades needed to make it available.

16.9.2 · Unlocking and acquisition

When both Attribute and Skill reach the required Grades, the Unique Trait unlocks automatically. No narrative feats, special Game Master authorisations or use counters are required.

Unlocking a Unique Trait does not mean acquiring it automatically. An unlocked Unique Trait can be acquired using the normal Talent choice granted by an Upgrade.

A Skill does not have to be a Role Skill to participate in a Synergy. A higher Grade automatically meets a lower requirement. If several Traits unlock simultaneously, all become available; each must be acquired separately.

16.9.3 · Stacking and Evolutions

Unique Traits can stack with one another, even when using the same Attribute or Skill. If a Trait is indicated as an Evolution of another, the new value replaces the previous one rather than adding to it.

Normal limits on Advantage and Disadvantage continue to apply. Traits grant no additional Actions, Bonus Actions or Reactions unless expressly stated.

16.9.4 · Relationship with Talents

A Unique Trait must not fully reproduce the effect of an existing Talent, Signature, Role Trait, racial ability, Haki effect or Prestige ability unless exceptions are expressly allowed in the catalogue. If a future revision creates a direct overlap, the Unique Trait is modified. Perfect Shadow’s overlap with the Sniper’s Shot from the Shadows is expressly allowed.

16.9.6 · Detailed catalogue

Athletics

Monstrous Body

Type: Passive · Requirements: Strength d12, Athletics d10

Your Passive Defence increases by +2.

The bonus stacks with other applicable modifiers to Passive Defence.

Living Fortress

Type: Passive · Evolution of Monstrous Body · Requirements: Strength d20, Athletics d12, Monstrous Body acquired

The Passive Defence bonus granted by Monstrous Body becomes +4.

The +4 replaces the previous +2 and does not stack with it.

Acrobatics

Superhuman Reflexes

Type: Passive · Requirements: Technique d12, Acrobatics d10 · Limit: once per round

When an attack against you misses your Passive Defence, you can immediately move 2 m.

The displacement is part of the Trait, uses no Reaction and does not provoke Reaction attacks caused by moving away.

Ghost Step

Type: Passive · Requirements: Technique d20, Acrobatics d12

When your Active Defence succeeds, you can immediately move up to half your Movement.

The displacement is part of the Trait and does not provoke Reaction attacks caused by moving away.

Observation

Eye of the Opponent

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Observation d10 · Limit: once per round

After a creature you can see makes an attack, you gain +2 to your next Active Defence against that creature before the start of your next turn.

The bonus applies to the Active Defence result, not Passive Defence.

Opening Predator

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Observation d12 · Limit: once per turn

When an enemy consumes their Reaction, your next attack against them before the end of the turn gains Advantage.

Impregnable Mind

Type: Passive · Requirements: Spirit d12, Observation d10

You gain Advantage on Spirit Saves made to resist fear, intimidation or mental coercion.

The Trait does not create a Save against effects that do not allow one.

Stealth

Silent Step

Type: Passive · Requirements: Technique d12, Stealth d10

When a Stealth Check is required while you are moving at no more than half your Movement, make the Check with Advantage.

Perfect Shadow

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Stealth d12

When you make an attack against a creature that has not detected you, that attack gains Advantage.

Limit: once per turn.

Survival

Wild Toughness

Type: Passive · Requirements: Strength d12, Survival d10

You gain Advantage on Saves required by heat, cold, exposure and other physical environmental dangers.

The Trait does not automatically cancel environmental effects that do not allow a Save.

Not Yet

Type: Passive · Requirements: Spirit d20, Survival d12 · Limit: once per encounter

When you fail a Strength or Spirit Save against a Status, you can increase the result by +2 after seeing the roll.

If the new result reaches the Threshold, the Save succeeds.

Communication

Silver Tongue

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Communication d10 · Limit: once per scene

When you fail a Communication Check made to persuade, negotiate or deceive, you can repeat the full roll.

You must accept the second result.

Overwhelming Presence

Type: Passive · Requirements: Spirit d12, Communication d12

Make Communication Checks used to command or intimidate with Advantage.

Medicine

Clinical Eye

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Medicine d10 · Limit: once per scene

When you achieve a success with a +4 margin on a Medicine Check made on a creature, your next Medicine Check on the same creature in the scene gains Advantage.

The benefit ends after the Check or at the end of the scene.

Surgical Hand

Type: Passive · Requirements: Technique d12, Medicine d12 · Limit: once per treatment

When a medical effect you use requires one or more dice to be rolled to determine HP recovered, you can reroll one of those dice.

You must accept the new result.

Mechanics

Mechanical Genius

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Mechanics d10

Make Mechanics Checks used to identify a malfunction or understand how machinery works with Advantage.

Watchmaker’s Hand

Type: Passive · Requirements: Technique d12, Mechanics d12

When a Mechanics Check uses Technique as its Attribute, add +2 to the Check result.

The bonus applies only to Mechanics Checks actually based on Technique.

Navigation

Sense of Course

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Navigation d10 · Limit: once per voyage

When you fail a Navigation Check, you can repeat the full roll.

You must accept the second result.

No Sea Can Stop Me

Type: Passive · Requirements: Spirit d12, Navigation d12 · Limit: once per voyage

When you fail a Navigation Check with a margin between -1 and -3, you can treat the margin as 0.

The Check is then treated as a barely successful result.

Exploration

Perfect Orientation

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Exploration d10

Make Exploration Checks used to orient yourself, find a route again or determine a direction with Advantage.

Explorer’s Step

Type: Passive · Requirements: Technique d12, Exploration d12 · Limit: once per turn

You can ignore up to 3 m of natural Difficult Terrain for Movement purposes.

The ignored stretch of terrain is crossed as normal terrain. Remaining Difficult Terrain follows the ordinary rules.

Nature

Naturalist Instinct

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Nature d10

Make Nature Checks used to recognise animals, plants, weather phenomena or natural dangers with Advantage.

Crafting

Master Artisan

Type: Passive · Requirements: Technique d12, Crafting d10

When you achieve a success with a +4 margin on a Crafting Check to make an object, the time required for that creation is halved.

The Trait does not reduce the object’s materials, costs or requirements.

Inventor

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Crafting d10

Make Crafting Checks used to design or modify an object with Advantage.

Carpentry

Heavy Hand

Type: Passive · Requirements: Strength d12, Carpentry d10

Make Carpentry Checks in which moving, holding in position or working heavy components is the main difficulty of the work with Advantage.

Structural Eye

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Carpentry d10

Make Carpentry Checks used to identify damage, failures or weak points in a structure with Advantage.

Cooking

Absolute Palate

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Cooking d10

Make Cooking Checks used to identify ingredients, spoilage or preparation errors with Advantage.

Cooking of the Soul

Type: Passive · Requirements: Spirit d12, Cooking d12

When a dish you prepared recovers Stamina, every creature receiving that recovery gains +1 additional ST.

A creature can receive this bonus only once from the same meal.

Art

Perfect Hand

Type: Passive · Requirements: Technique d12, Art d10

When an Art Check uses Technique as its Attribute, make the Check with Advantage.

Stage Presence

Type: Passive · Requirements: Spirit d12, Art d10

When you achieve a success with a +4 margin on an Art Check before an audience, the first Communication Check you make towards that same audience in the scene gains Advantage.

The benefit ends after the Check or at the end of the scene.

Archaeology

Historical Connection

Type: Passive · Requirements: Cunning d12, Archaeology d10

Make Archaeology Checks used to connect an artefact, location or inscription to information the character already knows with Advantage.

The Trait does not provide knowledge the character does not possess.

16.9.7 · Reference to Prestige

Synergies do not gain new Traits at individual intermediate Prestige Grades. When both components of the same Synergy reach Saikyō, that pair can make only one Prestige Unique Trait available according to the Prestige Manual rules. The Prestige Unique Trait catalogue will be defined separately; this rule does not automatically assign a new effect.
